[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 9.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 11073)\nTotal output lines: 3\n\n{"chunk_id":"5f75d3","wall_time_seconds":3.0200717,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2255,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"c5e248","wall_time_seconds":2.931415,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":7413,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 7413)\\nTotal output lines: 411\\n\\n---\\r\\ntitle: Teaching Voice\\r\\ntype: concept\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\\r\\ncreated: 2026-06-10\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nstatus: active\\r\\nsubject: Teaching Voice\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Teaching Voice\\r\\n\\r\\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core voice (every register)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\r\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\r\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\r\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\r\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\\r\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\"; \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\r\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\r\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\r\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\r\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\r\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The apology / accountability move\\r\\n\\r\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Hi Sandra,\\r\\n>\\r\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\r\\n> [link]\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Best,\\r\\n> Joshua\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\r\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\r\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\r\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\r\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\r\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\r\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\r\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\r\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Situational micro-templates\\r\\n\\r\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\r\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\r\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\r\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\r\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\r\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\r\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\r\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The three registers\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\r\\n\\r\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\r\\n\\r\\nReference line (target tone):\\r\\n\\r\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\\r\\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where i…1073 tokens truncated…ooking emails: hand over the link, not the pass settings (2026-09-08)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** \\"70 minutes each, and no expiration date, so there\'s no rush to use them.\\"\\r\\n**Keep:** \\"You\'re set up on LSAT U with 10 sessions.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nSession length and expiry are admin fields. The student sees them on the booking\\r\\npage when she picks a time, so putting them in the email is detail she did not ask\\r\\nfor and has to read past to reach the link. The credit count is worth saying because\\r\\nit tells her how much she has. Everything else about the pass stays in the backend.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** in a booking email, say who it\'s for, how many sessions, and the link.\\r\\nStop there.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Drill hub: say what the button does, not what the feature is called (2026-09-09)\\r\\n\\r\\nSix changes on `/classroom/drill`, from the run-3 pass. The hub named its own\\r\\nfeatures, and it assumed the student had rejected something.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Was | Now |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| \\"Start Smart Drill\\" | \\"Practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start One Passage\\" | \\"Read one passage\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start a Smart Drill instead\\" | \\"Or practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Read one passage instead\\" | \\"Or read one passage now\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open analytics\\" | \\"See where you lose points\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open journal\\" | \\"Review your missed questions\\" |\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Smart Drill\\" is the product\'s internal name for the adaptive set. On a button it\\r\\nasks the student to guess what she is about to get. The sentence above the button\\r\\nstill names it, which is where a product name belongs: after the thing has been\\r\\nexplained, not as the explanation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Instead\\" was the worse one. It tells the student she has just declined the\\r\\nassigned work by reading past it. She has not. \\"Or\\" offers a second path without\\r\\nnarrating a decision she never made.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Open analytics\\" and \\"Open journal\\" name a screen. A student does not want\\r\\nanalytics, she wants to know where the points are going.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fallback paragraph lost \\"an adaptive set that follows your weakest question\\r\\ntypes\\" for \\"the question types you miss most\\": the same fact, without the product\\r\\nvocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** a button says what happens next, in the student\'s words. Feature names\\r\\nbelong in the sentence that explains them, never on the control.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Words never to use in student copy\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the glossary. Internal names may stay in code, never on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Never | Use instead |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| beat, beats | step |\\r\\n| chunk | piece |\\r\\n| intake | plain instruction |\\r\\n| briefing, phase, tab, card | the thing\'s own name |\\r\\n| say it back | tell it back |\\r\\n| The Read | The Recall |\\r\\n| Recall Rephrase Colour | Read, Recall, Colour |\\r\\n| em dash, en dash | a full stop, or a colon before a list |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conditional Rephrase: name the student action, not the internal model (2026-09-15)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** diagram the statement on paper, choose the matching diagram, and organize\\r\\nthe map into four groups. “Required means right” is a separate takeaway because it\\r\\nexplains placement; it is not a fifth group.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Route” described the system’s representation, not the student’s work. Calling five\\r\\nunlike ideas rules also flattened the teaching structure and made the layout harder to\\r\\nread.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** name the action the student can see herself doing. Do not force a teaching\\r\\nreminder into the same hierarchy as the categories it explains.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [[teaching-voice]] · [[ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY]] · [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Session recaps: write a reusable reference, not a play-by-play (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** an opener saying the student got two questions right, followed by a summary of\\r\\nwhich answer they chose and how they explained it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** the concept the student needs tomorrow, a visual model of the reasoning, a\\r\\nshort diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nA student will not remember an isolated answer-choice exchange a day later, and the\\r\\nexchange is not useful unless it demonstrates a reusable rule. A recap should read like\\r\\na compact study note, not a record of the conversation. Name a question only when the\\r\\ndetails make the concept easier to apply. Praise belongs only when it identifies a\\r\\ndurable skill or meaningful change, not merely that an answer was correct.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** organize the recap around what the student can use next. Lead with the\\r\\nconcept, show the logic clearly, and finish with tangible practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A rule is not a fact (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”\\r\\nwithout checking whether the stimulus supplied a fact at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional\\r\\nrelationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say\\r\\nthat the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain\\r\\nthem. When a fact is present, apply a matching rule or a valid contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** label the role of each sentence before explaining the inference. Never\\r\\ninvent a fact to make a rule fire.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A post-session note is lecture material, not an email (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** a greeting, a dated recap heading, references to a question the student is\\r\\nexpected to remember, and a sign-off.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a concept title, a clear opening distinction, numbered procedures, spaced\\r\\nexamples, and practice that uses the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** the note should remain useful when the session itself is no longer fresh.\\r\\nIt must teach from the page instead of relying on remembered context.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe concept should not arrive as an abrupt list. Begin with a short explanation of\\r\\nwhy the distinction matters, show the full method at a glance, and then teach each\\r\\npath in the same order. Use tables for comparisons and numbered headings for a\\r\\nprocedure so the student can see the structure before reading the detail.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Define the concept in Joshua\'s language (2026-09-28)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “Some, most, and all tell you how much of a group fits a description.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** “Some means at least one. Most means more than half. All means every one,”\\r\\ngrounded in a concrete set of ten cookies before drawing conclusions from the words.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe cut line is vague and does not sound like Joshua teaching. It makes the reader\\r\\ndecode “fits a description” before learning any of the three words. It also risks\\r\\nblurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** open with the actual distinction or definition Joshua taught. Use a\\r\\nconcrete example to show what each statement proves, then introduce notation and\\r\\nthe inferences. Do not write an abstract umbrella sentence merely to announce the\\r\\ntopic.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Reference quality does not authorize reference topics (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua requested the Some, Most, and All note as a teaching-flow and visual-quality reference. Quantity examples and exercises do not belong in the separate facts/rules note. Remove that off-topic material; retain the distinction between what a rule says and what a fact establishes. Before future recaps, read the accumulated corrections in the Post Session Recap skill\'s `references/recap-calibration.md`. Preserve source-driven scope while matching the reference\'s teaching quality.\\r\\n\\r\\nAlgorithm       Hash                                                                   Path\\r\\n---------       ----                                                                   ----\\r\\nSHA256          1FA38AF6382CBC3F77EFE9785499EF5416F0C4EABFCC074370A7BB6C05EBDB7F       C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\dev\\\\ai-os\\\\raw\\\\in…\\r\\nSHA256          89A4F5266DC577F09EA99DE2A21B77C10D7887724D140E823A04C89B9E1129EE       C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\dev\\\\ai-os\\\\raw\\\\in…\\r\\n\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"a6dbe3","wall_time_seconds":2.7381653999999997,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2897,"output":"Keep useful material, relocate it to the concept it trains, and add missing depth. The current preview is implementation evidence, not a substitute for this new ordered skeleton.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Keep the definition, hypothetical emphasis, natural anchor and brief LSAT purpose in section 1. The guarantee/tendency comparison can be a short supporting explanation of the must relationship; it must not interrupt the sufficient-family-to-necessary progression.\\r\\n- Use the developed sufficient and necessary explanations in sections 2 and 4. Put ordinary-language family instruction in sections 3 and 5. Keep current expression activities but reserve full diagram-building for sections 8 onward.\\r\\n- Move the current arrow explanation out of the opening. Move reversal/containment teaching into the four-situation comparison after the conceptual contrapositive.\\r\\n- Add the conceptual contrapositive prediction and full four-case comparison before any symbolic contrapositive work. Existing later contrapositive material is a reuse candidate, not automatically approved opening content.\\r\\n- Keep target highlighting, language labs and clause-order examples for section 11. Existing micro-assignability is valuable; it does not require exposing a teaching-sequence catalogue to students.\\r\\n- Keep both named meaning checks. Introduce Personalized If/Then fully in section 12, with the What Test as Joshua\'s addition. Meaning readback remains useful in earlier demonstrations without teaching the entire later method out of order.\\r\\n- Keep recall and small checks directly after the idea they test. Put the integrated recorded diagram/contrapositive drill after all its prerequisites. Keep permission/instruction nuance as an additional boundary lesson after the core sequence, rather than between its foundational concepts.\\r\\n- Keep existing practice IDs, historical attempts and teacher grants intact. Renderer section order is not the same as assignment order. If new chapters require new identities or revised scope, add explicit versioned mappings and prove compatibility before changing live assignments.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Completeness and accuracy gates\\r\\n\\r\\nFor every row above, the authoring review must point to the exact note, worked example, student pause and fresh practice that teach it. A table listing words is not enough to establish fluency in each expression. Preserve all source concepts, their relative order and their explanatory purpose, while adding depth nearby.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s family names and Joshua\'s Group 1/2 lists differ. Cover both inventories contextually, without teaching an unsafe positional shortcut. In order to introduces the goal whose requirement is stated elsewhere; always can express a general relationship without identifying a universal necessary target in isolation. Distinguish only from the only using complete grammar and meaning.\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve the original rule when no immediate inference follows. Do not import the source\'s confidence-as-proof claim, fixed letter limit, unsupported timing generalizations or legacy Logic Games framing. Teach a practical meaning check and readable labels instead. The source does not supply its referred-to chapter answer key or the obscured line. No claims about those unseen contents.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe full source and all 13 skeleton sections received an independent read-only Codex comparison on 2026-10-03. It found no material missing conceptual beat; its diagram-construction-order precision was incorporated. This is source-completeness review, not Claude review or a verdict on a rendered rebuild.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Built local result, 2026-10-03\\r\\n\\r\\nThe existing `orientation` lesson now contains all 13 connected authoring sections in this order. The other three opening units remain available as targeted sufficient/necessary, Group 1 and Group 2 support. These sections do not create new assignment IDs, a student difficulty selector or a student curriculum catalogue.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe new renderer uses ordinary-language relationship exhibits, complete indicator-family explorers, an attempt-gated contrapositive prediction, four-case explorer, incremental original diagram, four sign-pattern checks, ordered practical FAQs, complete-target checks, both meaning checks and six fresh paired-diagram exercises. Permission/instruction boundaries follow part 13. The six new exercises are local formative interactions; they do not create recorded assessment attempts. The existing attached practice and saved-work contracts remain intact.\\r\\n\\r\\nVisual verification corrected selected-answer contrast and replaced the cramped phone IF/THEN composition with a stacked connection. All explanatory text survives manual/system reduced motion. Three browser passes cover desktop interaction/feedback/reset, responsive reading, and keyboard/motion behavior. Twenty width checks across four units have equal client/scroll widths. Evidence is in [the reference-sequence checkpoint](visual-proof/voice-application/2026-10-03-reference-sequence/).\\r\\n\\r\\nAn independent Codex source review found and resolved two learning defects: premature contrapositive explanation and predictable legend order in the integrated practice. The final ten-file suite passes 172 tests; the new eleven-test suite also passes after the type-only test correction. Production build and generation/preservation audits pass. Current proof is recorded in [VERIFICATION.md](visual-proof/voice-application/VERIFICATION.md). This is a local review build, not publication, fresh Claude approval, authenticated student acceptance or new media completion.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Historical opening-only plan, 2026-10-02\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s direction: **importance of conditionals -> sufficient and necessary conditions -> Group 1 / left-side indicators**. Develop each part together. Existing reports are reference material, not final approved lesson text. No application code changed.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotebook: [Conditional Logic | LSAT Logical Reasoning](https://notebook.google.com/notebook/e38c0aff-bb6e-498e-a5ef-a6b1bc3a6f5c).\\r\\n\\r\\n## The sources we actually found\\r\\n\\r\\nThe live source list contains three ready YouTube sources. No separate newly authored source titled Importance of Conditionals was visible at this check.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Conditional Logic | LSAT Logical Reasoning, source `37858ae5-c893-4b5b-9299-4b28c1706428`: applying rules to facts, chaining rules, NYC/USA example, triggers and requirements.\\r\\n- LSAT Logical Reasoning | Conditional Logic | Better Ways To Understand Conditional Statements, source `392fb8a1-cc3d-4a38-8e92-3615f424c0d0`: intuitive understanding through subsets, partial requirements, and multiple paths.\\r\\n- Master Conditional Logic in 30 Minutes | LSAT Logical Reasoning, source `384cb6b0-b79d-4b31-b3b7-b6d7dc783fad`: LR applications, sufficient/necessary meaning, examples, moving an if-clause without changing direction, indicator groups.\\r\\n\\r\\nAll three indexed source texts were retrieved through the authenticated CLI and reviewed. On-screen keyword charts are not fully preserved in their transcripts. Do not claim full visual-source transcription.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Match material to the first three lessons\\r\\n\\r\\n| Part | What the student needs to understand | Existing reference coverage | Proposed practice, for joint review |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Importance | A conditional tells us what is guaranteed, helping separate valid conclusions from assumptions. Its use reaches inference, flaws, assumption gaps, and matching argument structures. | LSAT Lab source explains rules-to-rules and rules-plus-facts; 7Sage source names LR applications. Interactive report introductions supply motivating examples. | Start with one rule and contrast a justified conclusion with a tempting extra assumption. Ask what the rule actually establishes. |\\r\\n| Sufficient and necessary | Sufficient is enough to guarantee the other condition; necessary is required for the first. A requirement does not by itself establish the trigger. | Both introductory sources define the roles. Better Ways adds the three mental models. The reports contain nested categories and one-way examples. | Keep one stable rule while asking which condition is enough, which is required, and what remains unknown. Use the existing nested-box activity where appropriate. |\\r\\n| Group 1 | Recognize language expressing a sufficient trigger, locate the complete ideas, and preserve direction when sentence order changes. | Sources and comprehensive study guide cover if, when/whenever, all, every, each, any. The core mechanics report also lists People who. Full indicator inventory must be reconciled with Joshua\'s notes. | Highlight complete trigger/result, explain direction, then translate independently. Mix front-loaded and end-loaded if-clauses. Include language that resembles an indicator but does not express a guarantee. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThis practice design is our recommendation; the sources do not establish its comparative effectiveness.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Existing reports inspected\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Mastering Conditional Logic for LSAT Logical Reasoning**: introduction, sufficient/necessary, four argument forms, indicator groups, chains. Opened and read in Codex.\\r\\n- **Mastering LSAT Conditional Logic: Triggers, Rules, and Requirements**: introduction, trigger/outcome cases, contraposition and compound terms, keyword translation. Opened and read in Codex.\\r\\n- **Mastering Conditional Logic: From Core Mechanics to Flawless LSAT Reasoning**: introduction, sufficient/necessary, three mental models, valid/flawed forms, keywords, chains. Opened and read in Codex; includes a slide deck embedded in its mental-model section and recommended artifacts elsewhere.\\r\\n- **Conditional Logic: Comprehensive Study Guide**: downloaded and reviewed. Supplies definitions, models, indicator table, and checks, but no dedicated importance-first opening.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese reports cover more than the first lesson. Extract relevant sections rather than assign the whole mixed report as an introduction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections before reuse\\r\\n\\r\\n- Numerical claims about question frequency or points lost are not established by the reviewed evidence.\\r\\n- The core mechanics report incorrectly gives Paris as a possible location within the USA. Correct that example before student use.\\r\\n- Some formulas render incorrectly in the newer report. Review visible output, not only underlying text.\\r\\n- Use \\"knowing the trigger is false does not determine the result\\" rather than saying the rule disappears.\\r\\n- Inclusion does not require one set to be strictly smaller. An arrow expresses implication, not necessarily causation or a chronological event.\\r\\n- Group assignments, unless shortcuts, and otherwise/biconditional claims require reconciliation with Joshua\'s authoritative notes and the complete sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Inspection side effect\\r\\n\\r\\nAn attempted browser opening of an existing report unexpectedly created an empty **New Note**. It was disclosed to Joshua in commentary, left blank, and preserved because deletion is not authorized. No artifacts were intentionally generated, edited, added, or shared. Report navigation is verified; the new note is not curriculum content.\\r\\n\\r\\nFeature research is saved separately at `research/2026-10-02-gemini-notebook-capabilities-report.md`. The two Firecrawl agents failed at their credit caps; official help pages and live browser inspection supplied the completed capability reference.\\r\\nwiki/sources\\\\conditional-reasoning-voice.md:37:The source explicitly distinguishes training from eventual performance: students write contrapositives in its drills to demonstrate the skill, even though the author does not recommend mechanically writing every contrapositive during a test.\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 9.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"13eb99","wall_time_seconds":4.0136575,"exit_code":1,"original_token_count":56,"output":"rg: raw/sources\\\\lsat-curriculum\\\\LSAT DUMP\\\\Main Conclusion : The system cannot find the file specified. (os error 2)\\nrg: raw/sources\\\\lsat-curriculum\\\\LSAT DUMP\\\\Videos : The system cannot find the file specified. (os error 2)\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"ef5380","wall_time_seconds":5.5099871,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2926,"output":"### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |\\r\\n| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that\'s the rule.” |\\r\\n| Inviting attention | let\'s look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |\\r\\n| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |\\r\\n| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |\\r\\n| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s browser corrections, 2026-10-03: define “guarantee” as the certainty supplied by the stated rule before using it to explain another idea. Explain “direction” through an actual relationship before using the word as advice. A beginner needs to see what the arrow means, which complete idea belongs on each side, and why sentence order need not match diagram order. “Read left to right” alone does not teach that. Introduce sufficient as enough and necessary as required through the example, then develop their limits.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor rule versus fact, make the hypothetical explicit: a rule tells us what would follow **if** a condition were true. It does not report that the condition happened. Contrast “Suppose this order is marked ready” with “This order is marked ready” before drawing a conclusion about an actual case. Keep the anchor sentence idiomatic and complete. Emphasis and motion around “if” should reveal that hypothetical relationship, with equally clear static information when motion is reduced.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 13.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"343962","wall_time_seconds":4.1863929,"exit_code":1,"original_token_count":0,"output":""}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"654142","wall_time_seconds":5.0818332999999996,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1798,"output":"Do not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n\\r\\nThe breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWithin the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntroduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nA repeated “Keep this” slogan without new reasoning is not explanatory repetition. Nor is rendering the same instructions twice in different panels. See the demonstrated corrections in [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Metaphor gives the reader a usable handle\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses doors, families, activation, burdens, brackets, and putting yourself in a condition\'s shoes. Each metaphor gives an abstract operation a physical or social handle. A small set of terms recurs, so the reader can keep using it.\\r\\n\\r\\nTransfer the method of choosing a metaphor, not the particular inventory. Prefer a familiar image that helps answer a logical question. Explain its literal meaning immediately. Remove it when it adds more things to remember than it clarifies.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor instance, a ticket can illustrate a requirement only if the rule is stated carefully. “A ticket is required to enter” does not say a ticket guarantees entry. An analogy fails if it smuggles in that reverse.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not build a competing metaphor for every sentence. Do not invent branded names for every skill. The standard technical terms remain the stable vocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Humor reduces tension without changing the rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s coffee obsession makes an unusual absolute rule memorable. Its reluctant-student aside acknowledges the experience of being asked to guess. The imagined green checkmark gives the reader\'s desire for certainty a recognizable shape.\\r\\n\\r\\nHumor can occur inside an explanation when it makes the thought easier to understand. Joshua\'s source selection supersedes the earlier blanket restriction that wit must stay outside the explanation. It does not authorize mockery, forced slang, exaggerated praise, or an expanding comic story.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse a light aside only when it helps the reader inhabit a situation or relax around a specific mistake. Keep it optional. A paragraph should still teach clearly if the aside is removed. Do not use the source\'s distinctive jokes as templates to be copied.\\r\\n\\r\\nNever turn first-person source anecdotes into Joshua\'s biography. “I ask every student this” requires that Joshua actually does. “Students always learn better this way” requires evidence or a narrower, truthful statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Emphasis and visuals identify the decision\\r\\n\\r\\nBold in the source often marks the rule, an inference, a warning, or an instruction. Italics distinguish examples and sometimes emphasize a word that changes the contrast. A table lets the reader compare controlled variations. A STOP asks the reader to attempt something. A side note answers a local question without interrupting the main line for everyone.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Bold the relationship or phrase that controls the next decision. Do not bold most of a paragraph.\\r\\n- Give an example its own space. The student should be able to find the sentence currently under discussion.\\r\\n- Put comparisons side by side when the same rule or wording is being contrasted.\\r\\n- Reveal diagram construction one meaningful step at a time. Explain each placement in English.\\r\\n- Preserve a readable text equivalent for every visual. The illustration is an explanation, not a substitute for one.\\r\\n- Make an interactive pause real. Let the learner choose when to reveal the reasoning.\\r\\n- Give a side note a specific job: answer a question, define new notation, or qualify advice. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Clear ordinary typography and meaningful labels should communicate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s adjusted HTML design is not a visual brand template. Our bootcamp should preserve the established Intro to LR student flow while using original visuals that serve the current concept. Student pages show the assigned work and the lesson itself; internal stage planning, assigned-start metadata, production notes, and selectable difficulty controls do not belong in the teaching prose.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The complete source progression and depth\\r\\n\\r\\nThis map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. The relationship comes before its parts\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"041440","wall_time_seconds":3.2587441999999998,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":174,"output":"- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\r\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\r\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\r\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\r\\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\\r\\n"}'}]

This is not proof of capture time.\n- Archived: 2026-10-03 America/Toronto.\n- Capture mechanism: user-supplied Markdown clip with timestamped transcript; extraction mechanism not independently verified.\n- Filename: `{filename}`\n- Size: {len(data)} bytes.\n- SHA-256: `{sha}`\n- Transformation: none. Exact bytes copied with metadata and verified. Inbox original retained.\n- Coverage: full supplied transcript read. No local screenshot or image attachment references occur in this clip. A YouTube embed, where present, is a video link, not an inspected image. No fresh video playback or board review claimed.\n''',encoding='utf-8')\n print(json.dumps({'file':filename,'sha256':sha,'bytes':len(data),'archive':str(dest)}))\n'@ | py -","max_output_tokens":1200}));

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/sources/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary.md\n+---\n+type: source\n+subject: LSAT Demon Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary\n+created: 2026-10-03\n+updated: 2026-10-03\n+aliases: []\n+tags: [lsat, conditionals, pedagogy]\n+sources:\n+  - ../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/LSAT Demon  Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary.md\n+---\n+\n+# A conditional rule does not say its trigger occurred\n+\n+Joshua supplied this LSAT Demon clip and requested its integration into the Conditional Bootcamp on 2026-10-03. The complete supplied transcript, 0:04 to 5:17, was read. The speaker is not identified in the clip. The [video URL](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_ho_yxw_Qqc) is present, but no fresh playback or visual-board inspection was performed. There are no local image references to inspect.\n+\n+## Preserved evidence\n+\n+- [Exact Markdown clip](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/LSAT%20Demon%20%20Understanding%20Sufficient%20v.%20Necessary.md), 3,854 bytes.\n+- [Provenance](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/PROVENANCE.md) and [hash manifest](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS).\n+- SHA-256: `1fa38af6382cbc3f77efe9785499ef5416f0c4eabfcc074370a7bb6c05ebdb7f`.\n+- Publication and capture dates unknown. Inbox last-write date was 2026-10-03. Archived and ingested 2026-10-03. Original retained unchanged; no matching archived hash or completed source summary found before intake.\n+\n+## What the source teaches\n+\n+| Timestamp | Source content, paraphrased | Useful teaching function |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| 0:04–0:26 | A conditional is a rule qualified by a condition, and several phrasings may state the same relationship. | Define the relationship before teaching vocabulary. |\n+| 0:51–1:31 | The speaker says a dropped pen will fall, then asks whether that establishes it will fall for sure. The pen has not been dropped. | Separate a hypothetical rule from a fact that satisfies it. |\n+| 1:48–2:15 | Sufficient means enough to establish the other condition. Necessary is what must hold when the sufficient condition holds. | Give each technical term an ordinary meaning and a stable example. |\n+| 2:40–2:59 | Sufficient belongs left and necessary right in the arrow representation. | Introduce notation only after explaining the roles. |\n+| 3:35–5:17 | If the pen did not fall, it could not have been dropped under the stated rule. The source calls this the contrapositive and emphasizes equivalence. | Ask for the consequence of a missing requirement before naming the operation. |\n+\n+The only explicit opening question asks whether the rule itself establishes that the pen will fall. Later reasoning is mainly a narrated contrapositive demonstration, not a supplied exercise bank. Do not claim the clip includes multiple-choice questions or a reviewed answer key.\n+\n+## How this belongs in Joshua's lesson\n+\n+The [existing 13-part source map](../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) still controls progression. This source supports deeper examples in the rule/fact, sufficient, necessary and conceptual-contrapositive sections. It does not replace the skeleton or Joshua's five construction steps. Use the [selected teaching voice](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md).\n+\n+Original adaptation: **If a display lamp is lit, its battery is installed.** First give only the rule and ask whether it establishes that the lamp is lit. Then add the fact that it is lit and ask what follows. Later, after explaining the necessary role, ask what follows if the battery is absent. Keep the same lamp and the same stated rule so the changing fact is the learner's focus.\n+\n+This is an authored hypothetical, not a technical claim about every real lamp. Introduce it explicitly as the rule for this exercise. Use fresh objects for independent practice after the walkthrough.\n+\n+## Boundaries and corrections\n+\n+- The source's phrasing that the rule is not “hard and fast” could suggest exceptions. Our conditional has no exception when its sufficient condition holds; what is conditional is its application to a case.\n+- A trigger need not cause an event, and a conditional need not describe a time sequence. The arrow means implication under the stated rule.\n+- Remove the source's weapon analogy and dismissive comparisons with schoolchildren. A learner's uncertainty deserves an explanation.\n+- The missing sufficient condition does not remove the rule. It leaves the other condition unsettled by that fact and rule alone.\n+- The clip is evidence of a teaching approach, not comparative learning-science evidence or proof of student outcomes.\n+\n+Related: [abstract-conditionals continuation](lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md), [LSAT Lab supplement](lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md), [selected voice source](conditional-reasoning-voice.md).\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/sources/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md\n+---\n+type: source\n+subject: LSAT Demon Abstract Conditionals\n+created: 2026-10-03\n+updated: 2026-10-03\n+aliases: []\n+tags: [lsat, conditionals, pedagogy]\n+sources:\n+  - ../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/LSAT Demon  Abstract Conditionals.md\n+---\n+\n+# Move from a familiar rule to an abstract relationship\n+\n+Joshua supplied this LSAT Demon clip and requested its integration into the Conditional Bootcamp on 2026-10-03. The entire supplied transcript, 0:00 to 9:10, was read. The closing fragments are incomplete. No source URL, speaker identity, local images or video embed are supplied. No unseen boards or missing speech were reconstructed.\n+\n+## Preserved evidence\n+\n+- [Exact Markdown clip](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/LSAT%20Demon%20%20Abstract%20Conditionals.md), 6,712 bytes.\n+- [Provenance](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/PROVENANCE.md) and [hash manifest](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS).\n+- SHA-256: `89a4f5266dc577f09ea99de2a21b77c10d7887724d140e823a04c89b9e1129ee`.\n+- Publication and capture dates unknown. Inbox last-write date was 2026-10-03. Archived and ingested 2026-10-03. Original retained unchanged; no matching archived hash or completed source summary found before intake.\n+\n+## What the transcript actually contains\n+\n+| Timestamp | Source content, paraphrased | Scope and use |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| 0:44–1:37 | Boston implies Massachusetts. Not being in Boston does not settle whether a person is in Massachusetts. Not being in Massachusetts excludes Boston. | Compare absent sufficient with absent necessary. Use an original familiar relationship. |\n+| 1:37–3:04 | A stipulated psychology/sociology rule can be accepted for the question even if real course registration works differently. The speaker offers several equivalent forms. | Teach students to reason from the supplied rule, without inventing an explanation for why it exists. |\n+| 3:27–3:56 | Swap the conditions and negate both to form the contrapositive. | Reserve the symbolic operation until the concept and notation have been taught. |\n+| 4:19–5:34 | Progress to X → ¬Y and its contrapositive Y → ¬X; paraphrase using only if and unless. | Negative conditions and abstract labels belong after the notation prerequisites. Unless stays in its later language lesson. |\n+| 6:06–7:36 | Critique unsupported conclusions from ¬Y, then use a smoking/health analogy to make the reverse error concrete. | Keep the counterexample technique; replace the medical example and check both proposed conclusions. |\n+\n+These are narrated prompts and demonstrations, not a formal supplied question set. The clip names student concerns about abstract rules. It does not provide a bank of standalone questions, choices or a reviewed answer key.\n+\n+## An explicit transcript discrepancy\n+\n+At 6:06 the transcript criticizes deriving **¬X** from **¬Y** under **X → ¬Y**. At 6:34 it criticizes deriving **X** from **¬Y**. These are different proposed conclusions. Both are unsupported, but the transcript shifts between them. We cannot identify whether this was a speaker correction or transcription error without playback.\n+\n+The logically correct teaching point is: given X → ¬Y and ¬Y, X could be true or false. Both X/¬Y and ¬X/¬Y satisfy the rule. Preserve the discrepancy as evidence; do not silently attribute a polished, consistent exercise to the source.\n+\n+## Original adaptations for the bootcamp\n+\n+Use the [existing source map](../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) and [selected voice](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md). These are newly authored examples inspired by the teaching functions above, not source quotations or published assignments.\n+\n+1. **Familiar inclusion:** Every square is a rectangle. A shape is not a square. Can it still be a rectangle? Yes. A rectangle with unequal adjacent sides supplies a possible case. Use only if these shape meanings are clear; the logic must not turn into a geometry test.\n+2. **Stipulated rule:** Every ticket in Drawer R has a blue border. A ticket is in Drawer R. What follows? It has a blue border. The exercise does not need to explain why tickets were sorted this way.\n+3. **Wording comparison:** “Only blue-bordered tickets are in Drawer R” and “A ticket is in Drawer R only if it has a blue border” both express Drawer R → Blue border. Ask learners to choose the matching relationship and explain what the sentence requires.\n+4. **Negative-condition transfer:** If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop. The room is not hosting a workshop. Does that settle whether a film is showing? No. Ask the learner to build two possible rooms: film/no workshop and no film/no workshop. Both fit. This directly demonstrates why the reverse inference fails.\n+5. **Delayed abstract retrieval:** After the negative-condition lesson, show X → ¬Y and ask which fact rules out X. Y does, because X would require ¬Y. Ask separately what ¬Y establishes about X: nothing definite. Keep the two questions distinct.\n+\n+## Do not import these source habits\n+\n+- Saying a rule no longer matters when one trigger is absent. Keep it available for later facts and other inferences.\n+- Profanity, ridicule or dismissive characterizations of a learner's reasoning.\n+- Smoking as a universally sufficient condition for an undefined “not healthy.” That analogy introduces vague and contestable content unrelated to learning the inference.\n+- Legacy Logic Games framing, source promotion or unsupported learning guarantees.\n+- The source's hurried “the only”/“must” sentence as a clean indicator template. Use a grammatical original and preserve obligation versus compliance.\n+- Unless/without before their transformations have been taught. Keep all topic prerequisites in the existing 13-part progression and later-unit boundaries.\n+\n+Related: [sufficient/necessary clip](lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary.md), [LSAT Lab supplement](lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md), [selected voice source](conditional-reasoning-voice.md).\n*** End Patch"));

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md\n@@\n Feature research is saved separately at `research/2026-10-02-gemini-notebook-capabilities-report.md`. The two Firecrawl agents failed at their credit caps; official help pages and live browser inspection supplied the completed capability reference.\n+\n+## LSAT Demon supplements, ingested 2026-10-03\n+\n+Joshua requested both newly clipped Demon notes for the continuing opening rebuild. [Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary](../../wiki/sources/lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary.md) and [Abstract Conditionals](../../wiki/sources/lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md) now have complete supplied-transcript analyses and immutable, hash-verified archives. Neither clip contains local screenshot attachments. This review does not claim video playback or inspection of unseen boards. Source wording and narrated prompts are recorded in those ledgers; the examples below are original adaptations for implementation.\n+\n+The 13-part supplied HTML remains the teaching skeleton. Demon adds ways to explain and practice the same ideas. It does not replace Joshua's selected voice, construction method, assignment boundaries or sequence.\n+\n+| Existing position | Original example or interaction | Prerequisite and teaching purpose |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| Conditional meaning, before roles | “For this exercise, if a display lamp is lit, its battery is installed.” Ask whether that rule by itself establishes that the lamp is lit. Then supply a lit-lamp fact. | Explain hypothetical rule versus case fact before drawing the consequence. The lamp rule is stipulated, not an engineering assertion. |\n+| Sufficient and necessary | Keep the lamp rule. Ask which fact is enough to establish the other, and which condition is required for a lit lamp. | Ordinary meaning first. Do not imply that implication always means causation or time order. |\n+| Conceptual contrapositive, then four situations | Change only the observed fact: battery absent. Ask whether the lamp can be lit. Later contrast battery installed and lamp unlit, each leaving two possible cases. | Required-side absence supports the contrapositive. Required-side presence and sufficient-side absence do not settle the other condition. |\n+| Group 1/2 wording and complete targets | “Every ticket in Drawer R has a blue border.” Compare “Only blue-bordered tickets are in Drawer R” and “A ticket is in Drawer R only if it has a blue border.” | Read the entire rule and identify the complete condition. A supplied sorting rule need not match a learner's real-world expectations. |\n+| Sign patterns and integrated practice | “If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop.” Give only no-workshop; ask the learner to construct two compatible rooms, one with a film and one without. | Negative conditions and symbolic reading already taught. The two cases expose the unsupported reverse by satisfying the rule while disagreeing about the proposed conclusion. |\n+| Later retrieval after notation | X → ¬Y: ask which fact rules out X, then separately what ¬Y settles about X. | Y rules out X; ¬Y leaves X open. Separate questions avoid conflating necessary presence with necessary absence. |\n+\n+Possible interaction forms include a fact switcher, two-world counterexample builder, equivalent-wording comparison and delayed reversible recall. These are design recommendations, not claims of proven comparative learning benefit. Place recall after the relevant explanation, require a genuine attempt before explanatory feedback, and include a fresh transfer example after a worked one.\n+\n+The Abstract Conditionals transcript shifts from rejecting ¬Y therefore ¬X at 6:06 to rejecting ¬Y therefore X at 6:34. Both conclusions are unsupported by X → ¬Y plus ¬Y; the ledger preserves the discrepancy. Do not label an adapted exercise a verbatim Demon question. Do not import ridicule, profanity, the vague smoking/health analogy, legacy Games framing, unsupported statistics, or wording that makes an inactive rule disappear. Unless/without stay in their later transformation lessons.\n*** Update File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/log.md\n@@\n*** End Patch"));
