Do not hand-edit generated types.\\r\\n- `supabase/functions/`: Edge Functions.\\r\\n- `supabase/migrations/`: database schema migrations.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Stack And Commands\\r\\n\\r\\n- Stack: React 18, Vite 5, TypeScript, Tailwind, shadcn/ui, Supabase, TanStack Query.\\r\\n- Package manager: `npm`. `package-lock.json` is canonical. Ignore stray Bun lockfiles.\\r\\n- Dev server: `npm run dev` on port `8080`.\\r\\n- Build: `npm run build`\\r\\n- Lint: `npm run lint`\\r\\n- Test: `npm run test`\\r\\n- Watch tests: `npm run test:watch`\\r\\n- Typecheck: `npm run typecheck`. Required before a push — `vite build` strips types without checking them, so a type error would ship. `.claude/safe-push.sh` gates on it.\\r\\n- E2E: `npm run test:e2e` (Playwright, `e2e/smoke.spec.ts`).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Working Rules\\r\\n\\r\\n- Keep changes small and aligned with existing patterns.\\r\\n- Use TypeScript React function components and the `@/` path alias.\\r\\n- Preserve Lovable-specific plumbing, including the `lovable-tagger` Vite plugin, the PWA setup, and the service-worker guard in `src/main.tsx`.\\r\\n- Do not change app behavior just to satisfy tooling setup.\\r\\n- Do not hardcode secrets or service-role credentials.\\r\\n- Treat Supabase-generated files as generated artifacts, not hand-edited source.\\r\\n- TypeScript strictness is intentionally loose. Do not tighten it without explicit approval.\\r\\n- Keep this adapter aligned with `CLAUDE.md` without duplicating the full project guide. Put durable detail in `CLAUDE.md`; keep shared reference material in `docs/`, `references/`, or `templates/`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Product Constraints\\r\\n\\r\\n- The Foyer is the primary navigation surface. The `GooeyDock` quick-launch row belongs only on `/foyer`.\\r\\n- Inbox is a real 1:1 student-admin messaging system with PDF attachments.\\r\\n- Admin surfaces must stay responsive on desktop and mobile.\\r\\n- Prefer truthful fallbacks over fake buttons, fake analytics, or placeholder behavior.\\r\\n- Onboarding asks for name, not username. Sign-in uses email. The instructor display name is `Joshua`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Smart Drill Note\\r\\n\\r\\nOn `/drill`, adaptive Smart Drill can show a `currentQuestion` that is not the same as `session.questionQueue[session.currentIndex]`. Any per-question UI state must key off `currentQuestion.qid` unless the code path is explicitly limited to queued drills.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Verification (must not skip)\\r\\n\\r\\n- Any change to `src/components/` or `src/pages/` is not \\"done\\" until it has been viewed running in the app (dev server + a browser tool), with a screenshot or DOM check as evidence. Never report a UI change complete on the strength of the diff alone. This is the single biggest recurring failure across past sessions — a hard gate, not a suggestion.\\r\\n- **Responsive fixes carry a wider gate: verify across the full emulate matrix BEFORE pushing, not after Joshua reports it.** Narrow, mid, and wide viewports plus the sidecar-docked case, every time. A pushed \\"fix\\" that does not hold costs a second full session; this has already happened once.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Workflow Expectations\\r\\n\\r\\n- Run `npm run build` before wrapping up significant code or documentation work.\\r\\n- If tests are relevant to the change, run them too.\\r\\n- After completing a change, stage the intended files, commit with a concise message, and push to `origin main` unless the user says otherwise.\\r\\n- **Push via `.claude/safe-push.sh`, not raw `git push`.** Lovable auto-commits to `main` mid-session, so a raw push can be rejected or race a concurrent commit. The script fetches, merges `origin/main` if it diverged (never rebases, never force-pushes), then pushes. It also gates on `npm run typecheck`.\\r\\n- **Never roll `main` back.** Do not use Lovable\'s \\"Revert to commit\\", \\"revert and resend\\", or history restore, and never reset or force-push `main`. On 2026-09-27 an accidental \\"revert and resend\\" (b998596) silently undid three merged PRs across 121 question-bank files, and students saw the wrong questions again. To undo your own work, `git revert <your SHA>` only. If a Lovable dialog offers a revert, dismiss it; if one fires, stop and tell Joshua before repairing anything.\\r\\n- Existing Codex repo hooks live in `.codex/hooks.json`; preserve them unless the task explicitly requires a hook change.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Session Protocol (second brain)\\r\\n\\r\\nProduct context, status, and decisions live in the Obsidian vault at `~/ai-os/`, not this repo. Two repo-local skills in `.agents/skills/` run the session ritual; the canonical protocol is in the vault `CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe vault was rebuilt 2026-07-16 (paths relative to `~/ai-os`). Live: teaching voice `wiki/teaching-voice.md` (route ALL student-facing copy through it), the business `wiki/entities/aspiring-attorneys.md`, a student\'s record `students/<name>/` (see `students/README.md`), teaching knowledge `corpus/index.md`, and the `wiki/{tasks,hot,dashboard,index,log}.md` hubs named below. Archived read-only reference (pre-rebuild product knowledge, may be stale): `archive/wiki-pre-rebuild-2026-07-16/domains/lsat-u/` — product context `…/synthesis/intro-to-lr-product.md`, design system `…/synthesis/intro-to-lr-design.md`, quality bar `…/synthesis/bootcamp-quality-bar.md`, domain index `…/index.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n- `pickup` — start of session. Read `wiki/tasks.md` (the Now / Next / Done ledger) first, then `wiki/hot.md`, `wiki/dashboard.md`, skim `wiki/index.md`, read the latest `wiki/log.md` entry. Emit a 6-8 line briefing (where we left off, Now, Next, open threads, hot, suggested next) and wait. Do not deep-read other pages until pointed at a task.\\r\\n- `wrapup` — end of session. Propose which `## Now` tasks finished and confirm before writing; a wrong \\"Done\\" is the failure to avoid. Then strike finished items through in `wiki/tasks.md` with a date, refresh `wiki/hot.md`, append a dated `wiki/log.md` entry (newest first, distinct label for same-day sessions), update the task anchor.\\r\\n\\r\\n`wiki/tasks.md` is the single source of truth for what is open vs done. Finished work is struck through so a new session never re-runs it. `raw/` in the vault is immutable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Codex Adapter Layout\\r\\n\\r\\n- `.codex/config.toml`: minimal shared Codex project config. Keep secrets and machine-specific overrides out of this file.\\r\\n- `.codex/agents/`: Codex agent definitions converted from `.claude/agents/*.md` when Claude agents exist. Use `.toml` files with converted instructions in `developer_instructions`.\\r\\n- `.agents/skills/`: repository-local Codex skills copied from `.claude/skills/` when Claude skills exist and Codex should use them. Keep each `SKILL.md` with its supporting files.\\r\\n- `.codex/hooks.json`: existing repository hooks. Preserve unless the task explicitly concerns hooks.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis checkout currently has Claude settings and hooks under `.claude/`, but no `.claude/skills/` or `.claude/agents/` directory to convert.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Known Gaps\\r\\n\\r\\n- Vitest specs exist (80 `src/**/*.test.ts(x)` files as of 2026-07-16); run with `npm run test`. Playwright is wired with one e2e spec (`e2e/smoke.spec.ts`), run via `npm run test:e2e`.\\r\\n- No Prettier config and no CI workflows.\\r\\n- `README.md` is still minimal.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Agent skills\\r\\n\\r\\n### Issue tracker\\r\\n\\r\\nGitHub Issues on `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db`, via the `gh` CLI. See `docs/agents/issue-tracker.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Triage labels\\r\\n\\r\\nThe five canonical roles, unchanged: `needs-triage`, `needs-info`, `ready-for-agent`, `ready-for-human`, `wontfix`. See `docs/agents/triage-labels.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Domain docs\\r\\n\\r\\nMulti-context: `CONTEXT-MAP.md` points at eight per-context glossaries; ADRs are all at the root. See `docs/agents/domain.md`.\\r\\n"}'}]

Byte preservation establishes unchanged implementations and tests, not fresh passing test results. The builder/parent owns execution and final functional proof.\n\n## Ongoing opening edits and limits\n\nSeven existing files are excluded from the protected set because the builder may revise them: ConditionalOpeningLesson.tsx, ConditionalOpeningLesson.review.test.tsx, ConditionalReferenceSequence.tsx, ConditionalReferenceSequence.test.tsx, opening.css, opening-polish.css and reference-sequence.css. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9503d9","wall_time_seconds":1.510051,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1021,"output":"---\\r\\nname: wrapup\\r\\ndescription: Close an LSAT U session by updating the Obsidian vault so the next chat picks up cleanly. Marks finished tasks done (struck through) in the task ledger, refreshes the handoff cache, and appends to the log. Trigger with /wrapup.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nClose the current LSAT U / Aspiring Attorneys session. Leave the next session a clean, accurate handoff and mark completed work as done so it is never re-run. Resolve the vault root from the current workspace and its nearest instruction files; never assume a saved `~/ai-os/`, Windows, or Mac path. In this vault, read `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` and obey its index/log conventions.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 0: read the anchor\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore writing anything, re-read `wiki/tasks.md` and `wiki/hot.md` so the handoff reflects the real goal, not just the last thing touched. If a `plans/` file or README framed this session, skim it too.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 1: confirm task status changes only when needed\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom verified session work, determine whether task status changes are needed. If no task status changes are needed, leave `wiki/tasks.md` alone and continue with the authorized handoff and log updates without a confirmation step. If task status changes are proposed, show Joshua the concrete proposal before editing `wiki/tasks.md`:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nProposing for wiki/tasks.md:\\r\\n  -> Done (strike through): <items you believe are finished this session>\\r\\n  -> Stays in Now: <still in progress>\\r\\n  -> New Next: <anything surfaced that should be queued>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nWait for Joshua to approve or correct proposed task status changes, unless those exact changes were already approved in this session. Completion MUST be correct: a wrong \\"Done\\" is the exact failure this prevents. Do not mark something done on a guess; if unsure, leave its status unchanged and say so. This gate applies to task status changes, not to routine authorized handoff/log filing. If requesting approval, explain that this skill requires confirmation before changing task status.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 2: write the authorized handoff and log\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **`wiki/tasks.md`** - move approved finished items into `## Done`, wrapping each in `~~...~~` with a date, e.g. `~~Wire AI tutor into official drill surface~~ (2026-06-12)`. Update `## Now` and `## Next` only as approved. Preserve older task records; do not prune or delete them without explicit authorization. Skip this file when no task changes were approved or needed.\\r\\n2. **`wiki/hot.md`** - rewrite the `## Most recent (YYYY-MM-DD): <title>` block with 4 to 8 bullets of what changed. Refresh `## Active priorities` and `## Open threads` so they are true now.\\r\\n3. **`wiki/log.md`** - append a new entry at the BOTTOM of the log using `## [YYYY-MM-DD] system | Session wrapup`. Include source/output links, meaningful changed pages, verified results, unresolved work, and any skipped steps. Use Joshua\'s client date/time context. Preserve old entries exactly, including their legacy formats; never rewrite or reorder them. Multiple sessions per day are normal, so add a distinct title suffix when useful. If this session\'s authorized handoff and log entry already contain the same verified work, report them rather than duplicating the entry.\\r\\n4. **Task anchor** - if a README, plan, or wiki page advanced, make a surgical status update. Touch only what changed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Step 3: confirm, then stop\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nHanded off: <1 line headline of this session>\\r\\nTasks: <N marked done, M still in Now>\\r\\nLogged: <the wiki/log.md entry label you added>\\r\\nNext session starts at: <the top open item in tasks.md / hot.md>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rules\\r\\n\\r\\n- No em dashes. Terse. Surgical changes only (Karpathy: touch only what changed).\\r\\n- `raw/` is immutable; never write there.\\r\\n- `pickup` is the mirror skill for session start; run it only when explicitly requested.\\r\\n- Read current target contents before writing. Preserve unrelated changes. Unattended automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections and uses compare-and-swap.\\r\\n- Do not re-log unchanged events, invent completed work, or claim a commit/push occurred without verification.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"641ebb","wall_time_seconds":1.4968451,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3518,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: operating-layer\\r\\nsubject: Hot last-session cache\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Hot — last-session cache\\r\\n\\r\\nFreshest working state; the first page `/pickup` reads. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 5.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"d53848","wall_time_seconds":1.680492,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2541,"output":"A misunderstanding of conditional statements is the most common source of error on the LSAT. Conditional statements are at the heart of logical reasoning. They’re also at the heart of how human beings make arguments in regular, day-to-day discussions. Here are some examples of conditional statements at work in daily life:\\r\\n\\r\\n- If I increase my LSAT score by a handful of points, I’ll be able to go to law school for free.\\r\\n- I want to go to bed early tonight; I don’t want to be too tired tomorrow morning.\\r\\n- If I fail this exam it’ll hurt my GPA.\\r\\n- He agreed to get us a table if he arrives at the restaurant first.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you’ve never studied formal logic, or if you hate it with a passion, don’t worry. Conditional statements are common sense. You just don’t know it yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RMAzOPvOHLw)\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Conditional Statement?\\r\\n\\r\\nA conditional statement is just an if-then statement like this one:\\r\\n\\r\\n`**If** you drink that poison, **then** you will get sick.`\\r\\n\\r\\nThe if-clause is called the “sufficient condition,” and the then-clause is called the “necessary condition.” If nothing else sticks from this lesson, remember this distinction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Sufficient Condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nLSAT test-writers refer to the if-clause as the “sufficient condition.”\\r\\n\\r\\nIt’s sufficient because it’s enough to guarantee that the then-clause will happen. You don’t need anything else. If you drink that poison, then, according to this rule, you will get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn real life, we might think of cases when people drink the poison and don’t get sick. There are always exceptions in life. But when you read an if-then statement on the LSAT, you have to take it at its word. It didn’t say that you might get sick or that most people get sick; it said that you will get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nRemember: [Accept premises, but attack conclusions](https://lsatdemon.com/resources/logical-reasoning/attacking-arguments)!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe if-clause is sufficient for triggering the then-clause. If the if-clause is triggered, then the then-clause has to happen. See what we did there?\\r\\n\\r\\n### Sufficient Condition Examples\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the following samples conditional statements, the if-clause is *italicized*:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. *If the sun spots continue to grow*, then we will lose all our crops this year.\\r\\n2. *If the car costs more than $2,000*, don’t buy it with cash.\\r\\n3. We can sell all our spiders *if we clean out their cages*.\\r\\n\\r\\nTake a moment to try and come up with your own examples.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Necessary Condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nTest-writers refer to the then-clause as the “necessary condition.”\\r\\n\\r\\nAbove, you learned that if the if-clause is triggered, then the then-clause must happen. The then-clause is necessary. If you drink the poison in the example above, you will get sick. There is no world in which you drink that poison and don’t get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice, however, that the reverse is not true. If you get sick, we can’t say for sure that you drank the poison. You could have. But you also could have gotten sick for some other reason.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe then-clause is not a sufficient condition because it does not guarantee that anything happened.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the same reason, the if-clause is not a necessary condition because it doesn’t always have to happen when something else happens. Keep reading to learn about how to avoid confusing a necessary condition with a sufficient condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Necessary Condition Examples\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the following sample conditional statements, the then-clause is **bolded**:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. If the sun spots continue to grow, **then we will lose all our crops this year**.\\r\\n2. If the car costs more than $2,000, **don’t buy it with cash.**\\r\\n\\t- Note: The word “then” doesn’t need to be explicitly stated. The word “if” is enough to signal a sufficient condition in the first clause, so the second clause must be the necessary condition.\\r\\n3. **We can sell all our spiders** if we clean out their cages.\\r\\n\\t- Note: The sufficient condition doesn’t always come first! Again, focus on the word “if.”\\r\\n\\r\\n## Confusing Sufficient with Necessary\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is one of the [LSAT’s most common flaws](http://www.thinkinglsat.com/articles/common-flaws), so make sure to learn it inside and out. Now that you understand the difference between a sufficient condition and a necessary condition, you should be able to spot instances when an argument confuses the two.\\r\\n\\r\\n![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AdogZX6Q5B8)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe names “sufficient” and “necessary” are important because most people think about if-clauses incorrectly. Consider the following silly if-then statement:\\r\\n\\r\\n`If you eat at Chipotle, then you will get a special tax credit.`\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat do you need to do to get that “special tax credit”?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf your gut reaction is “eat at Chipotle,” you’re not alone, but you’re misinterpreting the word “need.” Eating at Chipotle is the sufficient condition—eating there guarantees that you’ll get the special tax credit. But is that the only way to get the credit?\\r\\n\\r\\nEating at Chipotle might be the only way to get the credit, but there could be any number of other ways. Maybe you can get the credit by eating at McDonald’s or by just calling the IRS. There is not enough information in the argument to know. All you know for sure is that eating at Chipotle is one guaranteed way of getting the credit.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo, what do you need to do to get the credit? The short answer is that it’s unknown. The rule doesn’t tell us what’s necessary to get the tax credit. It tells us only what is sufficient to get that credit. Eating at Chipotle is sufficient for getting the tax credit. But the reverse is not necessarily true. Eating at Chipotle might not be necessary for getting the credit. You might be able to get the credit some other way.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you initially answered “eat at Chipotle” to the question above, you mistakenly turned the sufficient condition into a necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you are still struggling with this, watch our latest video on [sufficient vs necessary conditions.](https://lsatdemon.com/resources/demon-daily-podcast/sufficient-vs-necessary-conditions)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Known Unknowns\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen faced with a conditional statement, it’s important to be as aware of what you don’t know as you are of what you do know.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat you do know:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. You know what is sufficient to trigger the necessary clause (eating at Chipotle is enough to get the credit).\\r\\n2. You know the guaranteed result (getting the tax credit) of the sufficient clause (eating at Chipotle).\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat you don’t know:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. You don’t know what is sufficient to trigger the sufficient clause. Try to fill in the blank: “If \\\\_\\\\_\\\\_\\\\_, then you will eat at Chipotle.” You can’t! There might not even be a sufficient condition for eating at Chipotle. If you tried to fill the blank with “you get a special tax credit,” you mistakenly turned the necessary condition into the sufficient condition.\\r\\n2. You don’t know what is necessary to trigger the necessary clause. Try to fill in the blank: “If you get a special tax credit, then \\\\_\\\\_\\\\_\\\\_.” Again, you can’t fill in this blank! If you tried to say, “you ate at Chipotle,” you mistakenly turned the sufficient condition into the necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nWant to learn more about this topic? [Watch](https://youtu.be/YcAouFC1FSw) Ben discuss the difference between sufficient and necessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pop Quiz: Sufficient and Necessary Conditions\\r\\n\\r\\nUsing the rule below, answer each question:\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you sell your soul to a big law firm, then you will wallow in misery.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What is a sufficient condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\n2. What is a necessary condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\n3. What is a sufficient condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\n4. What is a necessary condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\n5. Which clause—if-clause or then-clause—is necessary for the other clause?\\r\\n6. Which clause—if-clause or then-clause—needs the other clause to happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answers**:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Selling your soul to a big law firm. This condition is a sufficient condition because if it happens, then you will definitely wallow in misery. (In the real world, of course, this is not true for every attorney—just, well, the vast majority.)\\r\\n2. We don’t know. The rule never says what is “necessary” for wallowing in misery; it only tells you what is “sufficient” for such wallowing. The if-clause is a sufficient condition. And although it could be a necessary condition as well, you simply don’t know because there might be other ways to get yourself to wallow in misery.\\r\\n3. We don’t know. The rule never says what is “sufficient” for selling your soul to a big law firm; it only tells you what is “necessary” for such a sale. The then-clause is a necessary condition. And although it could be a sufficient condition as well, you simply don’t know because the fact that you’re wallowing in misery does not guarantee that you sold (or will sell) your soul to a big law firm. Maybe your significant other just broke up with you after saying, “It’s not you. It’s me.”\\r\\n4. Wallowing in misery. This is a necessary condition because it has to happen if you sell your soul to a big law firm, at least according to this rule.\\r\\n5. Then. The then-clause is a necessary condition. It has to happen if the if-clause happens. The if-clause, however, doesn’t have to happen if the then-clause happens.\\r\\n6. If. This is tricky. Because the if-clause guarantees that the then-clause will happen, it needs the then-clause to happen. If you eat at Chipotle, then you have to get that credit. If you don’t get that credit, there’s no way that you could have eaten at Chipotle. The then-clause, however, doesn’t need the if-clause to happen. You could get the tax credit some other way.\\r\\n\\r\\nSign up for a [free account](https://lsatdemon.com/signup) to take advantage of our resources and start learning the difference now.\\r\\n\\r\\n<iframe allow=\\"clipboard-write; web-share\\" src=\\"chrome-extension://cnjifjpddelmedmihgijeibhnjfabmlf/side-panel.html?context=iframe\\"></iframe>\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"414364","wall_time_seconds":1.6456762999999999,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1908,"output":"raw/inbox\\\\settings.json\\nraw/inbox\\\\I Turned Claude Into the Ultimate Second Brain.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\Dashboard.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\Dashboard 2.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\Dashboard 1.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\Conditional Logic  LSAT Logical Reasoning.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\Claude Mods Is The Biggest Claude Code Upgrade Since Skills.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\Mastering Conditional Statements.md\\nraw/inbox\\\\LSAT Demon  Understanding Sufficient v. | Source | Location | Actual examples and questions |
|---|---|---|

Poison → sickness; restaurant meal → special tax credit; sunspots → crop loss; expensive car → instruction not to pay cash; cleaning spider cages → ability to sell spiders. Includes an actual six-question quiz with answers. |
| **Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary** | `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/raw/inbox/LSAT Demon  Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary.md` | 0:51–2:59: dropping a pen → falling, separating the rule from whether it happened. 3:35–5:17: no fall → not dropped. Identical archived copy already ingested. |
| **Abstract Conditionals** | `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/raw/inbox/LSAT Demon  Abstract Conditionals.md` | 0:44–1:37: Boston → Massachusetts, with Springfield as another possible location. 1:37–3:56: psychology → sociology despite arbitrary registration rules. 4:19–5:34: X → ¬Y. 6:06–7:36: unsupported reverse, explained through smoking/health. Identical archived copy already ingested. |

The missing article teaches a particularly useful distinction:

- **Source rule:** Eating at Chipotle guarantees a special tax credit.
- **Source question:** What do you *need* to do to get the credit?
- **Reason:** The rule gives one sufficient route. It never says this is the only route. Other routes could exist.
- **Original variation worth developing:** “If you win this raffle, you get a bicycle.” Winning is enough to establish that you get one. Getting a bicycle does not establish winning, because someone could buy or receive one another way. Ask what is enough **for what**, and what is required **for what**.

Its six-question quiz uses **working for a big law firm → misery**:

1. What is sufficient for misery? The stated law-firm condition.
2. What is necessary for misery? No further requirement established.
3. What is sufficient for the law-firm condition? Not established.
4. What is necessary for the law-firm condition? Misery, under the supplied rule.
5. Which clause is necessary for the other? Then-clause.
6. Which clause needs the other? If-clause.

That structure is stronger than memorizing labels. Adapt it to the raffle/bicycle example, carefully asking what **this rule establishes**, since the article’s unqualified “unknown” language overlooks trivial/self-implied conditions.

Other promising patterns:

- **Physical action:** Change the pen to dropping a coin. The learner understands the action and result immediately. State the assumed rule explicitly rather than debating real-world exceptions.
- **Ordinary alternative routes:** Winning a raffle versus buying the prize makes an invalid reverse easy to explain without geographical or technical knowledge.
- **Arbitrary rule after intuition:** A course-registration requirement teaches that the reason for the rule is irrelevant once supplied. Useful later, not as the first anchor.
- **Clause order:** Preserve the article’s method of moving the if-clause while holding the relationship fixed; replace its spiders/permission example with a cleaner claim.

Avoid importing the tax/legal assertions, poison/health claims, instruction-versus-compliance ambiguity, law-firm ridicule, or absolute “most common error” assertions.

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"82cdda","wall_time_seconds":1.9865731,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3966,"output":"A misunderstanding of conditional statements is the most common source of error on the LSAT. Conditional statements are at the heart of logical reasoning. They’re also at the heart of how human beings make arguments in regular, day-to-day discussions. Here are some examples of conditional statements at work in daily life:\\r\\n\\r\\n- If I increase my LSAT score by a handful of points, I’ll be able to go to law school for free.\\r\\n- I want to go to bed early tonight; I don’t want to be too tired tomorrow morning.\\r\\n- If I fail this exam it’ll hurt my GPA.\\r\\n- He agreed to get us a table if he arrives at the restaurant first.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you’ve never studied formal logic, or if you hate it with a passion, don’t worry. Conditional statements are common sense. You just don’t know it yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RMAzOPvOHLw)\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Conditional Statement?\\r\\n\\r\\nA conditional statement is just an if-then statement like this one:\\r\\n\\r\\n`**If** you drink that poison, **then** you will get sick.`\\r\\n\\r\\nThe if-clause is called the “sufficient condition,” and the then-clause is called the “necessary condition.” If nothing else sticks from this lesson, remember this distinction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Sufficient Condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nLSAT test-writers refer to the if-clause as the “sufficient condition.”\\r\\n\\r\\nIt’s sufficient because it’s enough to guarantee that the then-clause will happen. You don’t need anything else. If you drink that poison, then, according to this rule, you will get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn real life, we might think of cases when people drink the poison and don’t get sick. There are always exceptions in life. But when you read an if-then statement on the LSAT, you have to take it at its word. It didn’t say that you might get sick or that most people get sick; it said that you will get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nRemember: [Accept premises, but attack conclusions](https://lsatdemon.com/resources/logical-reasoning/attacking-arguments)!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe if-clause is sufficient for triggering the then-clause. If the if-clause is triggered, then the then-clause has to happen. See what we did there?\\r\\n\\r\\n### Sufficient Condition Examples\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the following samples conditional statements, the if-clause is *italicized*:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. *If the sun spots continue to grow*, then we will lose all our crops this year.\\r\\n2. *If the car costs more than $2,000*, don’t buy it with cash.\\r\\n3. We can sell all our spiders *if we clean out their cages*.\\r\\n\\r\\nTake a moment to try and come up with your own examples.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Is a Necessary Condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nTest-writers refer to the then-clause as the “necessary condition.”\\r\\n\\r\\nAbove, you learned that if the if-clause is triggered, then the then-clause must happen. The then-clause is necessary. If you drink the poison in the example above, you will get sick. There is no world in which you drink that poison and don’t get sick.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice, however, that the reverse is not true. If you get sick, we can’t say for sure that you drank the poison. You could have. But you also could have gotten sick for some other reason.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe then-clause is not a sufficient condition because it does not guarantee that anything happened.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the same reason, the if-clause is not a necessary condition because it doesn’t always have to happen when something else happens. Keep reading to learn about how to avoid confusing a necessary condition with a sufficient condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Necessary Condition Examples\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the following sample conditional statements, the then-clause is **bolded**:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. If the sun spots continue to grow, **then we will lose all our crops this year**.\\r\\n2. If the car costs more than $2,000, **don’t buy it with cash.**\\r\\n\\t- Note: The word “then” doesn’t need to be explicitly stated. The word “if” is enough to signal a sufficient condition in the first clause, so the second clause must be the necessary condition.\\r\\n3. **We can sell all our spiders** if we clean out their cages.\\r\\n\\t- Note: The sufficient condition doesn’t always come first! Again, focus on the word “if.”\\r\\n\\r\\n## Confusing Sufficient with Necessary\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is one of the [LSAT’s most common flaws](http://www.thinkinglsat.com/articles/common-flaws), so make sure to learn it inside and out. Now that you understand the difference between a sufficient condition and a necessary condition, you should be able to spot instances when an argument confuses the two.\\r\\n\\r\\n![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AdogZX6Q5B8)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe names “sufficient” and “necessary” are important because most people think about if-clauses incorrectly. Consider the following silly if-then statement:\\r\\n\\r\\n`If you eat at Chipotle, then you will get a special tax credit.`\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat do you need to do to get that “special tax credit”?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf your gut reaction is “eat at Chipotle,” you’re not alone, but you’re misinterpreting the word “need.” Eating at Chipotle is the sufficient condition—eating there guarantees that you’ll get the special tax credit. But is that the only way to get the credit?\\r\\n\\r\\nEating at Chipotle might be the only way to get the credit, but there could be any number of other ways. Maybe you can get the credit by eating at McDonald’s or by just calling the IRS. There is not enough information in the argument to know. All you know for sure is that eating at Chipotle is one guaranteed way of getting the credit.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo, what do you need to do to get the credit? The short answer is that it’s unknown. The rule doesn’t tell us what’s necessary to get the tax credit. It tells us only what is sufficient to get that credit. Eating at Chipotle is sufficient for getting the tax credit. But the reverse is not necessarily true. Eating at Chipotle might not be necessary for getting the credit. You might be able to get the credit some other way.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you initially answered “eat at Chipotle” to the question above, you mistakenly turned the sufficient condition into a necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you are still struggling with this, watch our latest video on [sufficient vs necessary conditions.](https://lsatdemon.com/resources/demon-daily-podcast/sufficient-vs-necessary-conditions)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Known Unknowns\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen faced with a conditional statement, it’s important to be as aware of what you don’t know as you are of what you do know.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat you do know:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. You know what is sufficient to trigger the necessary clause (eating at Chipotle is enough to get the credit).\\r\\n2. You know the guaranteed result (getting the tax credit) of the sufficient clause (eating at Chipotle).\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat you don’t know:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. You don’t know what is sufficient to trigger the sufficient clause. Try to fill in the blank: “If \\\\_\\\\_\\\\_\\\\_, then you will eat at Chipotle.” You can’t! There might not even be a sufficient condition for eating at Chipotle. If you tried to fill the blank with “you get a special tax credit,” you mistakenly turned the necessary condition into the sufficient condition.\\r\\n2. You don’t know what is necessary to trigger the necessary clause. Try to fill in the blank: “If you get a special tax credit, then \\\\_\\\\_\\\\_\\\\_.” Again, you can’t fill in this blank! If you tried to say, “you ate at Chipotle,” you mistakenly turned the sufficient condition into the necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nWant to learn more about this topic? [Watch](https://youtu.be/YcAouFC1FSw) Ben discuss the difference between sufficient and necessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pop Quiz: Sufficient and Necessary Conditions\\r\\n\\r\\nUsing the rule below, answer each question:\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you sell your soul to a big law firm, then you will wallow in misery.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What is a sufficient condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\n2. What is a necessary condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\n3. What is a sufficient condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\n4. What is a necessary condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\n5. Which clause—if-clause or then-clause—is necessary for the other clause?\\r\\n6. Which clause—if-clause or then-clause—needs the other clause to happen?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answers**:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Selling your soul to a big law firm. This condition is a sufficient condition because if it happens, then you will definitely wallow in misery. (In the real world, of course, this is not true for every attorney—just, well, the vast majority.)\\r\\n2. We don’t know. The rule never says what is “necessary” for wallowing in misery; it only tells you what is “sufficient” for such wallowing. The if-clause is a sufficient condition. And although it could be a necessary condition as well, you simply don’t know because there might be other ways to get yourself to wallow in misery.\\r\\n3. We don’t know. The rule never says what is “sufficient” for selling your soul to a big law firm; it only tells you what is “necessary” for such a sale. The then-clause is a necessary condition. And although it could be a sufficient condition as well, you simply don’t know because the fact that you’re wallowing in misery does not guarantee that you sold (or will sell) your soul to a big law firm. Maybe your significant other just broke up with you after saying, “It’s not you. It’s me.”\\r\\n4. Wallowing in misery. This is a necessary condition because it has to happen if you sell your soul to a big law firm, at least according to this rule.\\r\\n5. Then. The then-clause is a necessary condition. It has to happen if the if-clause happens. The if-clause, however, doesn’t have to happen if the then-clause happens.\\r\\n6. If. This is tricky. Because the if-clause guarantees that the then-clause will happen, it needs the then-clause to happen. If you eat at Chipotle, then you have to get that credit. If you don’t get that credit, there’s no way that you could have eaten at Chipotle. The then-clause, however, doesn’t need the if-clause to happen. You could get the tax credit some other way.\\r\\n\\r\\nSign up for a [free account](https://lsatdemon.com/signup) to take advantage of our resources and start learning the difference now.\\r\\n\\r\\n<iframe allow=\\"clipboard-write; web-share\\" src=\\"chrome-extension://cnjifjpddelmedmihgijeibhnjfabmlf/side-panel.html?context=iframe\\"></iframe>\\r\\n| Existing position | Original example or interaction | Prerequisite and teaching purpose |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Conditional meaning, before roles | “For this exercise, if a display lamp is lit, its battery is installed.” Ask whether that rule by itself establishes that the lamp is lit. Then supply a lit-lamp fact. | Explain hypothetical rule versus case fact before drawing the consequence. The lamp rule is stipulated, not an engineering assertion. |\\r\\n| Sufficient and necessary | Keep the lamp rule. Ask which fact is enough to establish the other, and which condition is required for a lit lamp. | Ordinary meaning first. Do not imply that implication always means causation or time order. |\\r\\n| Conceptual contrapositive, then four situations | Change only the observed fact: battery absent. Ask whether the lamp can be lit. Later contrast battery installed and lamp unlit, each leaving two possible cases. | Required-side absence supports the contrapositive. Required-side presence and sufficient-side absence do not settle the other condition. |\\r\\n| Group 1/2 wording and complete targets | “Every ticket in Drawer R has a blue border.” Compare “Only blue-bordered tickets are in Drawer R” and “A ticket is in Drawer R only if it has a blue border.” | Read the entire rule and identify the complete condition. A supplied sorting rule need not match a learner\'s real-world expectations. |\\r\\n| Sign patterns and integrated practice | “If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop.” Give only no-workshop; ask the learner to construct two compatible rooms, one with a film and one without. | Negative conditions and symbolic reading already taught. The two cases expose the unsupported reverse by satisfying the rule while disagreeing about the proposed conclusion. |\\r\\n| Later retrieval after notation | X → ¬Y: ask which fact rules out X, then separately what ¬Y settles about X. | Y rules out X; ¬Y leaves X open. Separate questions avoid conflating necessary presence with necessary absence. |\\r\\n\\r\\nPossible interaction forms include a fact switcher, two-world counterexample builder, equivalent-wording comparison and delayed reversible recall. These are design recommendations, not claims of proven comparative learning benefit. Place recall after the relevant explanation, require a genuine attempt before explanatory feedback, and include a fresh transfer example after a worked one.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Abstract Conditionals transcript shifts from rejecting ¬Y therefore ¬X at 6:06 to rejecting ¬Y therefore X at 6:34. Both conclusions are unsupported by X → ¬Y plus ¬Y; the ledger preserves the discrepancy. Do not label an adapted exercise a verbatim Demon question. Do not import ridicule, profanity, the vague smoking/health analogy, legacy Games framing, unsupported statistics, or wording that makes an inactive rule disappear. text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/sources/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements.md\n+---\n+type: source\n+subject: LSAT Demon Mastering Conditional Statements\n+created: 2026-10-03\n+updated: 2026-10-03\n+aliases: []\n+tags: [lsat, conditionals, pedagogy]\n+sources:\n+  - ../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/Mastering Conditional Statements.md\n+---\n+\n+# One sufficient route need not be the only route\n+\n+Joshua requested all clipped LSAT Demon conditional material for intuitive original examples. This generic-title article was found after the two explicitly named Demon clips. Unlike those transcripts, it contains a six-question quiz with a supplied answer key. The entire supplied article and answer key were read on 2026-10-03.\n+\n+## Preserved evidence\n+\n+- [Exact clip](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/Mastering%20Conditional%20Statements.md), 10,036 bytes.\n+- [Provenance](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/PROVENANCE.md) and [SHA-256 manifest](../../raw/sources/clips/lsat-demon-mastering-conditional-statements-2026-10-03/SHA256SUMS).\n+- SHA-256: `814c346c022473d0f3ac362060969c2aec76a1fa5ec0a577466e2776ffec9061`.\n+- Publisher attribution: LSAT Demon, supported by first-party links and signup invitation. Individual author, canonical article URL, publication date and actual capture date are unknown. Related linked pages are not evidence of the canonical article URL. Inbox last-write date was 2026-10-03; original retained unchanged.\n+- No matching archived hash/source summary existed before this intake. No local images occur in the clip. Embedded YouTube links were not played, and the browser-extension iframe was not executed.\n+\n+## What the article does\n+\n+The article starts with ordinary conditional language and defines sufficient as enough to establish the other condition under the supplied rule. Its poison/sickness anchor demonstrates that a sufficient condition guarantees the necessary condition without making the reverse inference valid. It asks readers to accept a supplied premise rather than invent real-world exceptions.\n+\n+Its most useful teaching passage supplies a deliberately artificial restaurant-meal/tax-credit rule. It then asks what someone **needs** to do to receive the credit. The tempting answer mistakes a guaranteed route for the only route. Other routes could fit the rule. This distinguishes “enough for the result” from “required for the result” using an ordinary question rather than labels alone.\n+\n+The article also demonstrates if-clauses at the end of sentences and omitted “then.” Its examples concern crops, a car-payment instruction and ability to sell spiders after cleaning cages. These are evidence of the article's method, not recommended student-facing examples.\n+\n+## The supplied six-question structure\n+\n+The article uses a satirical law-firm/misery conditional. Abstract it as A → B for source analysis:\n+\n+| Question function | Supplied answer, paraphrased | Precise interpretation for adaptation |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| What is sufficient for B? | A. | A establishes B under the supplied rule. |\n+| What is necessary for B? | Unknown. | The rule does not establish that A is required for B. Do not claim absolutely no necessary conditions exist. |\n+| What is sufficient for A? | Unknown. | The rule does not establish that B is enough for A. Do not deny trivial or other independently supplied sufficient conditions. |\n+| What is necessary for A? | B. | A cannot hold while B is false under the rule. |\n+| Which clause is necessary for the other? | The then-clause. | B is required for A. |\n+| Which clause needs the other? | The if-clause. | A needs B. This wording tests the learner's understanding from the opposite grammatical perspective. |\n+\n+The distinction between the last two questions is particularly useful: **B is required for A** and **A requires B** describe the same relationship. Ask both naturally without turning the exercise into a vocabulary trick.\n+\n+## Original adaptation authorized for development\n+\n+Joshua approved an original raffle example modeled on this teaching pattern: **If you win this raffle, you get a bicycle.** The relationship is supplied for the exercise, not a claim about every raffle. Winning is enough to establish getting a bicycle. Getting a bicycle alone does not establish winning this raffle: receiving one as a gift or buying one could be consistent alternatives.\n+\n+Keep the alternatives as possible cases, not new premises about a particular person. Scope each question to what this rule establishes. “Does this rule make winning the raffle necessary for getting a bicycle?” is clearer than asking for an unrestricted inventory of every necessary condition for obtaining a bicycle.\n+\n+After the roles have been explained, vary the supplied fact and ask what follows, then ask the learner to describe a bicycle recipient who did not win the raffle. Later contraposition can ask whether someone who never gets a bicycle could satisfy the winning condition under this same rule. Avoid ambiguous snapshots such as “doesn't have the bicycle yet,” which do not deny eventual receipt. The [existing source map](../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) owns placement. The [selected teaching voice](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) continues to govern prose.\n+\n+This records an authorized example direction, not a claim that an app revision, assignment or publication has occurred.\n+\n+## Limits and corrections before reuse\n+\n+- Unsupported prevalence claims, scholarship/tax implications, medical assertions and the law-firm caricature are not imported into student material.\n+- The article's statement that a then-clause is not sufficient and an if-clause is not necessary is too absolute. A supplied A → B does not establish B → A; another rule could establish it. Name the missing inference rather than declaring the roles impossible.\n+- “Nothing can fill the blank” overstates what is unknown. Restrict the question to the relation between the two named conditions established by this rule.\n+- Distinguish instructions and permission/ability from observed compliance. A car-payment instruction does not prove how someone paid; ability to sell does not prove a sale.\n+- A premise accepted for an exercise need not be causal, chronological or independently plausible. Do not turn every conditional into a physical trigger.\n+- The article supplies examples and teaching advice, not comparative learning-science evidence. Do not claim measured learning gains from adopting its question pattern.\n+\n+Related: [Understanding Sufficient v. Necessary](lsat-demon-sufficient-necessary.md), [Abstract Conditionals](lsat-demon-abstract-conditionals.md). These are three distinct clipped sources. Inbox/archive copies of the first two were hash-identical; this third article was previously unarchived.\n*** End Patch"));

This supersedes the earlier strict relative order where needed while preserving substantive concepts. The sidebar correction alone did not solve over-fragmentation.\\r\\n\\r\\n### What Intro to LR actually does\\r\\n\\r\\nCurrent primary path in src/bootcamps/main-conclusion/content/introToLrPath.ts has 13 actions: 8 teaching notes, 3 standalone labs and 2 six-question practice sets. Its order is:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. The Three Parts of an LR Question\\r\\n2. So, An Argument\\r\\n3. Premises\\r\\n4. Conclusions\\r\\n5. Five Types of Conclusions\\r\\n6. Conclusion Type Lab\\r\\n7. Beyond Premises and Conclusions\\r\\n8. The 2-Part Conclusion Check\\r\\n9. Main Conclusion Lab\\r\\n10. First-Sentence vs Rebuttal Structures\\r\\n11. First-Sentence vs Rebuttal Lab\\r\\n12. First-Sentence Main Conclusion (6 questions)\\r\\n13. Rebuttal Main Conclusion (6 questions)\\r\\n\\r\\nIt does not put a standalone drill after every note. IntroLessonPage.tsx renders seven answer/check widgets across four of the eight notes: premise indicator choices, support choice, conclusion indicator choices, an eight-item indicator classification quiz, sentence conclusion selection, five conclusion-type classifications and the two-part conclusion check (three claims with two yes/no judgments each). Anatomy scanning, support visualization, five explanatory type cards and four definition reveals are different interaction categories, not additional independent quizzes. Imported but unrendered role-labeling components are not counted. The older introGuidedSequence.ts workbook and routed reference/flashcard/simulator extras are not included in the current primary-path count.\\r\\n\\r\\nConclusion Type Lab has 12 items and requires a category choice before revealing feedback. Main Conclusion Lab requires selecting and analyzing a sentence and a correct result before Next. First-Sentence vs Rebuttal Lab has 20 stimuli and four reasoning stages, but also has an explicit assisted Skip/Reveal All route. IntroNotesShell can mark a note complete on Next without requiring embedded checks. Therefore neither every interaction nor every completion checkmark proves independent mastery. Adopt the substantive teaching/application sequence, not these completion assumptions.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntro LR comparison is code-based. Its live reference route was assignment-gated, and no student completion/usage claim is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Why the conditional opening has 70 pages\\r\\n\\r\\n| Current unit | Pages | Main source of excess |\\r\\n|---|---:|---|\\r\\n| Orientation | 26 | Covers nearly the whole opening; one diagram stage split into three pages and six exercise pairs split into six pages, plus separate recall/lab/repair destinations. |\\r\\n| Sufficient/necessary | 9 | Repeats definitions, relationships, four cases and foundation recall already introduced in orientation. |\\r\\n| Group 1 | 17 | Two introductions, seven expression pages, six recurring method/practice pages, recall and repair. |\\r\\n| Group 2 | 18 | Same structural pattern plus mixed-wording practice. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe problem is not that everything is unnecessary. The navigation gives explanations, examples, reminders and individual questions equal weight, while the following units repeatedly restart teaching. Keep useful depth; combine overlapping explanation and turn repetition into retrieval or application.\\r\\n\\r\\nExisting practice inventory: 25 unique reviewed cards (4 foundation,10 Group1,11 Group2) with29 placements because foundation is repeated;14 additional legacy recall examples;14 target examples;14 diagram-builder examples; four-case explorer; three target-position examples; four sign-pattern questions; six original/contrapositive exercise pairs; three applied labs for counterexample construction, negative cases and equivalent wording. Recorded banks remain separate from this reading-page count. Do not imply there is no practice code because the preview fails to interleave it visibly.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Recommended smaller path\\r\\n\\r\\nNine reading pages and four practice checkpoints, arranged as follows:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What conditionals are. Rule versus fact, guarantee versus usually, approved raffle example, LSAT purpose.\\r\\n2. The sufficient condition. Enough for what; winning is enough for getting a bicycle, but not the only possible route.\\r\\n3. The necessary condition. Required for what; required result does not mean earlier prerequisite or sufficient on its own.\\r\\n4. Practice checkpoint: rule/fact, enough/required, fresh case and a counterexample.\\r\\n5. Read the must arrow. Sufficient left, necessary right; the left requires the right. Compare if-clause first/last without changing the arrow. Sentence position is flexible; arrow roles are not.\\r\\n6. Sufficient-condition words. One grouped lesson with all seven core expressions and supplementary forms; in-page named examples/attempts rather than seven destinations.\\r\\n7. Practice checkpoint: identify the whole sufficient condition, build a diagram, explain or repair its direction.\\r\\n8. Necessary-condition words. One grouped lesson retaining all expressions, extended requirement wording and obligation/permission versus compliance.\\r\\n9. Keep the whole meaning when wording changes. Complete conditions, only/the only, target position, What Test and Personalized If/Then as applications.\\r\\n10. Practice checkpoint: mixed families and unfamiliar sentences, with qualifications and modality.\\r\\n11. What a missing requirement tells you. Conceptual contrapositive and four cases, including what stays open and settled outcome versus still waiting.\\r\\n12. Write and read the contrapositive. Negate both whole conditions and reverse; existing negatives/sign patterns. Practical FAQ embedded as optional help.\\r\\n13. Practice checkpoint: all six paired original/contrapositive exercises in one item-by-item sequence, retaining feedback and answers.\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery teaching page can retain a brief meaningful check. Flashcards follow prerequisites within lessons; a flip is not treated as an assessed response. Full practice checkpoints should require an attempt, explain wrong choices and provide retry/repair. This proposal does not yet specify a new mastery gate or change student completion policy.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep: complete definitions, reasoning, original examples, indicator nuances, explanations of errors and useful practice. Combine: repeated introductions, arrow/word-order subpages, duplicated method explanations and recall pages. Move: six individual exercise pages into one practice destination, FAQs beside the relevant activity, mistake recaps into answer feedback. Remove: duplicate transitions and repeated recaps that add no reasoning task.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Implementation boundary\\r\\n\\r\\nCurrent material belongs to four unit/progress contracts. A coherent compressed path must preserve assignments, historical responses, recorded banks and difficulty. Do not silently reinterpret old completions. No app edits were made in this audit. This recommendation is the next curriculum correction, not a claim that the current preview already has thirteen stages.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2026-10-04 live reference correction: mirror the course, not the reference rail\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua opened https://lsatprep.study/bootcamp/intro-to-lr/lessons and explicitly identified its structure as the intended model. Parent now inspected that authenticated live course index, the Premises note and Conclusion Type Lab landing page. This supersedes the earlier reference-rail interpretation and the prior statement that live inspection was unavailable (that limitation applied to the earlier local route/session).\\r\\n\\r\\nVerified structure: persistent course-level sidebar with Lessons under Intro Path; Practice Drills and Drill Lab under Practice; tools such as Flashcards under Tools. Lessons index is a vertical ordered series of cards labeled NOTE, DRILL or PRACTICE, each with a clear title, one purpose sentence and Read Lesson/Start Drill/View Instructions action. Conclusion Type Lab has its own12-question entry page. No live question was answered and no Mark Complete action was clicked.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrect conditional target: course-level navigation and a Lessons index containing separate concept lessons and interleaved drill cards. What conditionals are, Sufficient condition and Necessary condition must each be genuine dedicated lessons. Keep examples, quick checks and recall within the relevant lesson. Use separate drill destinations for substantive practice transitions. Do not retain a70-page sidebar inside orientation or relabel micro-pages as lessons. The9lesson+4practice proposal describes course destinations, not nested reader pages. Match the clean card hierarchy and restrained layout of the supplied live index; preserve approved conditional copy and persistence/assignment semantics rather than copying IntroLR\'s completion side effects.\\r\\n\\r\\nNo application changes in this live analysis. Earlier sidebar implementation remains current but is superseded as the desired structural model.
