original: {item.readback}</p><p>Read its contrapositive: {item.contraReadback}</p></div>}</div>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function conditionalReferenceContent() {\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-sequence\\">\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={1} label=\\"Start with meaning\\" title=\\"What does a conditional tell you?\\">\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is an idea that can be true or false. You win a raffle. You get a bicycle. We can ask whether either condition holds in a particular case.</p>\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> tells us that if one condition is true, another has to be true too. It gives us a rule we can use, even before we know whether either condition is true in a particular case.</p>\\r\\n      <p className=\\"co-if-inline\\">We aren\'t saying the first condition happened. We\'re asking what would follow <strong>if</strong> it did.</p>\\r\\n      <HypotheticalFigure/>\\r\\n      <h3>Why learn this?</h3><p>On the LSAT, you need to distinguish what the information proves from what it leaves open. A conditional rule can help you prove a conclusion. An argument can also misuse the rule and reach a conclusion it hasn\'t proved. We\'ll practice telling those apart.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Let\'s read one rule.</p><blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>Under this rule, winning the raffle makes getting a bicycle certain. Winning without getting one would break the stated relationship. That is what we mean by a <strong>guarantee</strong> here.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Compare that with “If you win the raffle, you usually get a bicycle.” Usually allows an exception. Our rule does not. Take the stated rule as given; we are asking what it tells us, not whether a real organizer keeps a promise.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Does the rule itself tell us that you actually win the raffle?\\" options={[\'Yes, it says I win.\', \'No, it tells me what follows if I win.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You added a case fact. The rule has not reported a win; it tells you what getting one would establish.\', \'Imagining a win helps us understand the rule. We need a separate fact to establish that you actually win.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Now let\'s take the two conditions one at a time.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={2} label=\\"Sufficient means enough\\" title=\\"One condition gives you enough\\">\\r\\n      <blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote><p>The <strong>if-part</strong> is the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Sufficient means enough. Knowing you win is enough, under this rule, to know you get a bicycle.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-role-exhibit\\"><span>Sufficient</span><strong>Win the raffle</strong><p>Enough to establish getting a bicycle</p></div>\\r\\n      <p>You might win. You might not. The rule has not settled that question.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"You don\'t win the raffle. Could you still get a bicycle?\\" options={[\'Yes. Getting a bicycle is still possible.\', \'No. Without winning, getting a bicycle is impossible.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'You could buy one or receive one as a gift. You could also not get one. Neither case creates a winner who fails to get a bicycle, so both fit the rule.\', \'You made winning the only way to get a bicycle. The sentence gives one sufficient condition; it has not excluded other ways.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Now suppose a fact gives us the sufficient condition. That is when we can <strong>use</strong>, or activate, the rule in its stated direction.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"You win the raffle. Do we need another fact to establish that you get a bicycle?\\" options={[\'Yes, we need another fact about the bicycle.\', \'No, the rule and the win establish it.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'The rule already covers the case in which you win. That fact meets its sufficient condition.\', \'Winning meets the sufficient condition. With the rule, that is enough to establish that you get a bicycle.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>We\'ll now keep this job in mind while changing the wording.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={3} label=\\"The sufficient family\\" title=\\"Different words can preserve the same rule\\">\\r\\n      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps you recognize a conditional relationship. These expressions can introduce an idea that is enough to establish another.</p>\\r\\n      <p>For the next examples, use a different stated rule: anyone who borrows a locker has a key. We will express that same connection in several ways. We haven\'t said everyone with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n      <WordFamily entries={sufficientFamily} role=\\"sufficient\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>When/whenever and any/anytime group related forms together. An ending changes the kind of case being discussed, but these examples keep the same connection. Read the whole sentence. A familiar word is a clue, not a substitute for its meaning.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Every guest wearing a blue badge receives a lunch voucher. Which complete idea is enough?\\" options={[\'Guest\', \'Guest wearing a blue badge\', \'Receives a lunch voucher\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Guest alone drops the badge restriction and would make a claim about unbadged guests.\', \'The rule gives a voucher to every guest wearing a blue badge. Keep that qualification with the person.\', \'The voucher is what the rule establishes. It has not said every voucher recipient wears a blue badge.\']}/>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension\\"><summary>Two more Group 1 expressions</summary><p><strong>Where</strong> can state a relationship across locations: “Where a collection point operates, parcels can be returned.” An operating collection point establishes the availability of returns there. Availability is not proof that someone made a return.</p><p><strong>The only</strong> needs its complete phrase: “The only notebooks on this shelf are hardback.” A notebook on the shelf is hardback. The sentence does not put every hardback notebook there.</p><p>These are part of your Group 1 work. Understand what the full expression covers before using its group as a placement shortcut.</p></details>\\r\\n      <p>We have seen what can be enough. Now let\'s name what that condition requires.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={4} label=\\"Necessary means required\\" title=\\"The other condition has to be there\\">\\r\\n      <blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote><p>The <strong>then-part</strong> is the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. Under this rule, if you win, getting a bicycle has to follow.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-role-exhibit cr-required\\"><span>Necessary</span><strong>Get a bicycle</strong><p>Required whenever you win the raffle</p></div>\\r\\n      <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, a required result of winning under this rule. It is not a requirement you must satisfy before entering. The word necessary describes the relationship, not when the events occur.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'re looking at the same rule from its other side. Winning is enough to establish getting a bicycle. Getting a bicycle is the result the rule requires whenever you win.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Under our rule, what has to follow if you win?\\" options={[\'You get a bicycle.\', \'You ride the bicycle home.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Getting a bicycle is the stated result. Winning without getting one would violate the rule.\', \'Riding is not part of this rule. The sentence establishes that you get a bicycle, not what you do with it.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Necessary does not mean “the next event.” It means required by this relationship. A conditional need not tell you which event caused another, or when either happened.</p>\\r\\n      <p>As with sufficient language, the wording can vary while the relationship stays the same.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={5} label=\\"The necessary family\\" title=\\"Listen for what the sentence needs\\">\\r\\n      <p>Return to our locker rule. Borrowing a locker requires a key. The next expressions all identify that requirement in their complete sentences.</p>\\r\\n      <WordFamily entries={necessaryFamily} role=\\"necessary\\"/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-contrast\\"><div><h3>If</h3><p>“You borrow a locker <strong>if</strong> you have a key.”</p><p>Having a key is enough for borrowing under this sentence.</p></div><div><h3>Only if</h3><p>“You borrow a locker <strong>only if</strong> you have a key.”</p><p>Having a key is required. This sentence has not made it enough.</p></div></div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A guest enters the lounge only if the guest has a current pass. What does entering establish?\\" options={[\'The guest has a current pass.\', \'Every guest with a pass enters.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Only if identifies the current pass as required for entry. An entering guest therefore meets that requirement.\', \'You made the requirement enough. A guest could have a current pass and remain outside without violating the sentence.\']}/>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension\\"><summary>More Group 2 forms, with the sentence intact</summary><p><strong>Only when</strong> and <strong>only where</strong> can restrict the occasions or places of an outcome: “The fountain runs only when its pump is on.” A running fountain requires an operating pump. The pump being on does not establish that the fountain runs.</p><p><strong>Requires</strong> directly names a requirement: “Entry requires a current pass.” Preserve current. <strong>Always</strong> needs the sentence\'s subject and scope: “The upstairs room is always quiet when the door is shut.” Here a shut door establishes quiet, not the other way around.</p><p><strong>Only</strong> and <strong>the only</strong> are different complete expressions. “Only people who get a bicycle win this raffle” and “The only people who win this raffle are people who get a bicycle” both preserve the rule: winning establishes getting a bicycle.</p></details>\\r\\n      <p>We can now recognize both roles before writing any symbols. Next, let\'s see what happens when a requirement is missing.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={6} label=\\"A missing requirement\\" title=\\"What if the requirement is missing?\\" dark>\\r\\n      <ContrapositiveDiscovery/>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={7} label=\\"The four situations\\" title=\\"Keep what follows separate from what stays open\\">\\r\\n      <p>Change the fact about your outcome while keeping the rule. Treat each fact as settled, not as a report that you are still waiting. Decide what it establishes before comparing the explanation.</p>\\r\\n      <FourCaseExplorer/>\\r\\n      <p>Starting from getting a bicycle and concluding that you win is the tempting <strong>reverse</strong>. Starting from not winning and concluding that you do not get a bicycle is the tempting <strong>inverse</strong>. Neither follows from this rule alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The contrapositive starts from <strong>not getting a bicycle</strong> and establishes <strong>not winning the raffle</strong>. Its reasoning depends on the missing required result, not on reversing whichever words happen to appear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We now understand the relationship in words. Symbols will let us record that understanding without carrying every word of the sentence.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={8} label=\\"Build the first diagram\\" title=\\"Give the relationship a shorter form\\">\\r\\n      <p>Put the sufficient condition on the <strong>left</strong>, the necessary condition on the <strong>right</strong>, and a <strong>must arrow</strong> between them.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Sufficient: enough\\" right=\\"Necessary: required\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>The arrow means the left condition requires the right. It does not mean the left caused the right, or that the right happens later.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Which idea goes on which side?</h3>\\r\\n      <p>The indicator helps you find a condition\'s job. Once you\'ve found that job, place the <strong>complete condition</strong> on its side of the arrow. We don\'t put the word if or only if in the diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-contrast\\"><div><h3>Left: sufficient</h3><p>This is the condition that\'s enough to establish the other one. Group 1 expressions such as <strong>if, when, all, every</strong> and <strong>any</strong> can identify this condition.</p><p>“All people who borrow a locker have a key.” All refers to <strong>people who borrow a locker</strong>. For someone in that group, having a key follows. Borrowing goes left.</p></div><div><h3>Right: necessary</h3><p>This is what the sufficient condition requires. Group 2 expressions such as <strong>only if, requires</strong> and <strong>must</strong> can identify the requirement.</p><p>“Borrowing a locker requires a key.” What is required? <strong>Having a key.</strong> That condition goes right.</p></div></div>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Borrows a locker\\" right=\\"Has a key\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Read it back: if someone borrows a locker, they have a key. The arrow records that connection. It hasn\'t told us that everyone with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.</h3>\\r\\n      <p>Compare these two versions of our raffle rule. The indicator is bold; the complete idea it refers to is in brackets.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>If</strong> [you win the raffle], you get a bicycle.</p><p>You get a bicycle <strong>if</strong> [you win the raffle].</p><span>If identifies the sufficient condition in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>In the second sentence, if appears in the middle. Its condition comes last, but winning is still enough to establish getting a bicycle. That condition still goes left. Don\'t let the first phrase in English choose the first side of your diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Win the raffle\\" right=\\"Get a bicycle\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Now try the same move with a requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>Only</strong> [people with a key] borrow a locker.</p><p>A person borrows a locker <strong>only if</strong> [they have a key].</p><span>Only and only if identify the necessary condition here.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Both sentences say locker borrowers have a key. In the first, only is at the beginning. In the second, only if is in the middle. Having a key is required in both, so it goes right. <strong>If</strong> and <strong>only if</strong> have different jobs; read only if as the whole expression.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A person borrows a locker only if they have a key. Which condition goes on the right?\\" options={[\'Borrows a locker\', \'Has a key\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Borrowing is what has the requirement. Ask what a locker borrower must have: a key. Borrowing goes left; having a key goes right.\', \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Only if appears in the middle, but it still identifies the necessary condition.\']}/>\\r\\n      <h3>The indicator can also follow its target</h3>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The target is the complete idea the indicator refers to. It comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. We find it by asking what is required, not by taking whichever words come next.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You\'ll work through the less familiar expressions in their Group 1 and Group 2 lessons. For each one, keep the whole sentence: what is enough, and what does it require?</p>\\r\\n      <p>Let\'s build an original diagram slowly. First find the indicator, then draw the arrow, place the complete idea it refers to, and fill the other side.</p>\\r\\n      <IncrementalWriter/>\\r\\n      <p>Now move the if-clause: “You get a bicycle if you win the raffle.” The winning condition comes last in English but still goes left in the diagram. Its job has not changed.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Which diagram preserves the moved if-clause?\\" options={[\'Get bicycle → Win\', \'Win → Get bicycle\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You let sentence order choose the arrow. The if-clause still makes winning enough for getting a bicycle.\', \'The sufficient condition is still winning. Where it appears in the sentence does not change what it establishes.\']}/>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={9} label=\\"Write and read the contrapositive\\" title=\\"Write the meaning you already understand\\">\\r\\n      <p>Win means you win the raffle. Get bicycle means you get a bicycle. Our original is Win → Get bicycle.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We already know that not getting a bicycle rules out winning. Write that as <strong>~Get bicycle → ~Win</strong>. The <strong>~</strong> sign means <strong>not</strong>. Not getting the outco…5120 tokens truncated…ich the sentence rules out.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Winning is enough to establish getting a bicycle, and getting a bicycle is required whenever you win. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Win the raffle: enough to establish getting a bicycle.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Get a bicycle: required whenever you win.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\\r\\n        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between winning and getting a bicycle.</p>\\r\\n        <GateExplorer/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\\">\\r\\n        <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Enters the archive\\" right=\\"Has a pass\\"/>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Necessary doesn\'t mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we\'d need information establishing that additional connection.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?\\" options={[\'Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.\', \'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.\', \\"You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn\'t a finalist to receive an invitation too.\\"]}/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>For this rule, imagine yourself in the raffle. Keep the same raffle and the same bicycle outcome in mind on both sides.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If I win the raffle, what do I know?\\">\\r\\n          <p>I get a bicycle. The rule covers the case in which I win. Win the raffle → Get a bicycle matches what the sentence tells me.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If I get a bicycle, must I have won the raffle?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. I could get a bicycle without winning by buying one or receiving a gift. That fits the rule because it does not create a winner who fails to get a bicycle.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Get a bicycle → Win the raffle would make every bicycle receipt proof of winning. The sentence does not give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Read the roles in the relationship\\">\\r\\n        <p>In <strong>Win the raffle → Get a bicycle</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it is enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it is required whenever the left holds.</p>\\r\\n        <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don\'t ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We\'ll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === \'group-1\' ? \'g1\' : \'g2\'))!;\\r\\n  const first = id === \'group-1\';\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the sufficient idea\' : \'Find what is required\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. We\'ll work through the meaning of each expression, then practice finding its complete target.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-vocabulary\\">{group.words.map(word => <span key={word}>{word}</span>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps us recognize the relationship. Its <strong>target</strong> is the complete idea it refers to. Finding that idea matters more than taking the next few words in the sentence.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Before looking for a word, ask what the sentence says. Does it give a rule, cover every member of a group, promise a result, or name something required? Each can tell us that if one condition holds, another must hold. A sentence about what usually happens leaves room for exceptions; a conditional rule makes its consequence certain whenever its sufficient condition holds.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read the arrow as <strong>must</strong>. The sufficient condition goes on the left. The necessary condition goes on the right. If the left condition is true, the right condition must be true too. The arrow doesn\'t say which event happened first, and it doesn\'t establish that one event caused the other.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Different wording can make the same rule\' : \'Read it as a requirement\'}>\\r\\n      {first ? <>\\r\\n        <p>Suppose a studio has this rule: every painting with a silver seal has been inspected. Having the seal is enough to establish inspection. Let\'s keep that meaning and change the wording.</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>If a painting has a silver seal, it has been inspected.</blockquote><p>If what? It has a silver seal. That complete idea goes on the left.</p></div><div><blockquote>A painting has been inspected whenever it has a silver seal.</blockquote><p>Whenever what? It has a silver seal. The sufficient condition comes later in this sentence, but it still goes on the left.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Has silver seal\\" right=\\"Has been inspected\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>The indicator can appear at the beginning or in the middle. Sentence position doesn\'t choose the side of the arrow. Find the idea the expression refers to, then ask what the sentence tells you if that idea is true.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Each painting with a silver seal has been inspected. Did changing every to each change the rule?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Each still covers every painting with that seal. A painting with the seal that hadn\'t been inspected would break either statement. Keep has silver seal on the left and has been inspected on the right.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Some wording presents a rule: <strong>if, when, whenever</strong>. Some covers a whole group: <strong>all, each, any, every</strong>. Read each in its sentence. Any in “Do you have any paint?” asks a question; it doesn\'t give us a rule.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Other sentences promise an outcome: “A silver seal ensures the painting has been inspected” or “A silver seal implies the painting has been inspected.” Here, the promised outcome is inspection. Ensures and implies express the arrow between the two complete ideas. Leads to, will, and always can also express a definite result in a rule, but the word alone doesn\'t settle the meaning. “The studio will open tomorrow” gives us no sufficient condition to put on the left.</p>\\r\\n        <p>We\'ll now examine the Group 1 expressions individually. Keep asking what each sentence makes sufficient, rather than sorting words without their meaning.</p>\\r\\n      </> : <>\\r\\n        <p>A useful way to read the whole rule is: <strong>the left condition requires the right condition</strong>. Suppose borrowing a studio camera requires a current membership. What is required for borrowing? A current membership. That\'s the necessary condition.</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>Borrowing a studio camera requires a current membership.</blockquote><p>The required idea comes after requires. Find the whole idea: a current membership.</p></div><div><blockquote>A current membership is required to borrow a studio camera.</blockquote><p>The same required idea now comes first. Moving it in the sentence hasn\'t changed what borrowing requires.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Borrows studio camera\\" right=\\"Has current membership\\"/>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a current membership. Does this rule tell us she borrowed a camera?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Membership is something borrowing requires. Mina could have the membership and never borrow a camera. The rule tells us what we need if she borrows; having what she needs doesn\'t tell us she did.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Notice the meaning shared by <strong>requires, necessitates, depends on,</strong> and <strong>relies on</strong> when they express a strict requirement. If borrowing depends on having a current membership, borrowing still requires membership. “Depends partly on” may describe an influence instead, so read the whole claim before treating it as a conditional.</p>\\r\\n        <p>You may also hear the requirement as “In order to borrow a camera, you must have a current membership.” The goal is borrowing; the required condition is membership. Only and only if can set the same restriction. We\'ll work through their wording next.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Using the recording booth depends on having a reservation. What belongs on the right?\\" options={[\'Uses recording booth\', \'Has a reservation\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Using the booth is what needs the reservation. Putting use on the right would make a reservation enough to establish that someone used the booth, which the sentence never says.\', \'A reservation is required for using the booth, so it belongs on the right. Having a reservation still leaves open whether the person uses it.\']}/>\\r\\n        <p>You won\'t always see a familiar word. Find what the sentence says is required <strong>for the other condition</strong>. Then check that your arrow keeps that requirement on the right.</p>\\r\\n      </>}\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    {IndicatorSections({ id })}\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the target, then place it\' : \'Find the requirement, then place it\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"Add what to the indicator when that gives you a sensible question. If what? All what? The only what? Your answer should be the whole idea the expression refers to. Then check whether that idea really is enough to establish the other condition.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"Ask what the indicator refers to. Only what? Only if what? What is required? The target can come before or after the expression, so check its meaning in the complete sentence.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <p>This is the <strong>What Test</strong>. The question helps you find the indicator\'s complete target before choosing its side of the arrow. Keep any words that limit which people, objects, places, or occasions the sentence covers. The question finds the idea; the whole sentence tells you what that idea does.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Work through the expressions below. First choose the complete target. Then place it on its side of the arrow and read the relationship in ordinary English.</p>\\r\\n      <TranslationLab id={id}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Keep the whole condition\\" dark>\\r\\n      <blockquote>{first ? \'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.\' : \'Entry requires a current pass.\'}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"Applicant alone isn\'t the condition. The sentence is about an applicant who submits late. Keep that restriction when you shorten the idea; otherwise your diagram would make a claim about on-time applicants too.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. Shortening a label should save writing while preserving what the sentence requires.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <Check prompt={first ? \'What goes left in this rule?\' : \'What goes right in this rule?\'} options={first ? [\'Applicant\', \'Applicant submits late\'] : [\'Has current pass\', \'Has any pass\']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first\\r\\n        ? [\'You dropped the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would cover every applicant, including someone who submits on time. The sufficient idea is applicant submits late.\', \'This keeps the complete sufficient idea. The rule covers applicants who submit late, so keep that restriction with the applicant.\']\\r\\n        : [\'This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.\', \'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the sentence requires.\']}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Read the only as a complete expression\' : \'Compare only with the only\'}>\\r\\n      {first ? <>\\r\\n        <blockquote>The only people admitted are members.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The only what? The only people admitted. The complete target is admitted people. If someone is admitted, the sentence tells us they\'re a member.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Admitted\\" right=\\"Member\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>A member who stays outside fits this rule. Nothing says every member is admitted. Check that your diagram keeps that possibility open.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"The only notebooks kept on this shelf are hardback. Which diagram preserves the rule?\\" options={[\'Hardback → Kept on shelf\', \'Kept on shelf → Hardback\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You made being hardback enough for a place on the shelf. The sentence limits the notebooks kept there; it does not put every hardback notebook on this shelf.\', \'A notebook kept on this shelf has to be hardback. A hardback notebook on another shelf would also fit the rule, so this direction keeps what the sentence actually says.\']}/>\\r\\n      </> : <>\\r\\n        <p>Try the distinction on the raffle rule we already understand. The wording changes, but winning still tells us that you get a bicycle.</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>Only people who get a bicycle win this raffle.</blockquote><p>Only refers to people who get a bicycle. Getting one is required whenever someone wins, so get a bicycle goes right.</p></div><div><blockquote>The only people who win this raffle are people who get a bicycle.</blockquote><p>The only refers to people who win. Winning is enough to establish getting a bicycle, so win the raffle goes left.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Win the raffle\\" right=\\"Get a bicycle\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>Both sentences preserve the same direction. Someone who gets a bicycle as a gift without winning fits both. Neither sentence makes getting a bicycle enough to establish winning.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Only registered guests receive wristbands. What follows from receiving a wristband?\\" options={[\'The person is a registered guest.\', \'Nothing about registration.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Only limits wristbands to registered guests, so receiving one establishes that the person is registered.\', \'You treated the sentence like all registered guests receive wristbands. Only restricts who receives one, so the wristband does establish that the person is a registered guest.\']}/>\\r\\n      </>}\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"From sentence to diagram\\">\\r\\n      <p>You have seen the expressions with help. Now use a new sentence. Identify both complete ideas, choose the sufficient one for the left, and check whether your diagram says what the sentence says.</p>\\r\\n      <p>If you know the meaning but hesitate over the arrow, imagine the left condition is true. Does the sentence then require the right condition? Read the whole relationship in English. If it makes a promise the original didn\'t make, change the direction.</p>\\r\\n      <DiagramBuilder id={id}/>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"co-practical-note\\"><summary>Why write a diagram when I understand the sentence?</summary><p>For one short sentence, you may be able to hold the connection in your head. Here, writing it makes your direction and complete conditions visible so you can check them. If your first diagram doesn\'t match the meaning, find the exact phrase you misread and revise that part.</p></details>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Say the rule without the clues\\">\\r\\n      <p>Read the new sentence and explain its direction before revealing. If you hesitate, ask what the indicator refers to, then try the proposed relationship in ordinary English. Compare your explanation with the one that appears.</p>\\r\\n      <Flashcards id={id}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Check the meaning, not just the word\\">\\r\\n      <Check prompt={first ? \'A report is reviewed if submitted before noon. Which is the same rule?\' : \'A student graduates only if the project is completed. Which is the same rule?\'} options={first ? [\'If reviewed, it was submitted before noon.\', \'If submitted before noon, it is reviewed.\'] : [\'If the student graduates, the project was completed.\', \'If the project is completed, the student graduates.\']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first\\r\\n        ? [\'You reversed the if relationship. Submission before noon is enough for review; the sentence has not said review proves when the report was submitted.\', \'This preserves the if-clause as the sufficient condition. Submission before noon gives us review even though it appears second in the original sentence.\']\\r\\n        : [\'This preserves project completion as a requirement for graduation. It does not turn completing the project into a promise of graduation.\', \'You turned the only-if requirement into a guarantee. Graduation requires completion, but the sentence allows a completed project without graduation.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>The guided practice asks you to write your reasoning with the method beside you. Then the attached drill gives you fresh questions. Use the explanation to inspect the direction and complete conditions, not only whether you chose the same answer.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction OpeningFigure({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {\\r\\n  if (unitId === \'orientation\') return <figure className=\\"co-hero-figure co-meaning-figure\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">What follows if a condition is true</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-hero-sentence\\">What would we know <strong>if</strong> this were true?</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-hypothetical-tag\\">A situation to imagine</div>\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 3.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"87e81f","wall_time_seconds":1.6344764999999999,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":6220,"output":"      <SignPatterns/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-method\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">The complete five-step routine</p><ol><li><strong>Find the indicator.</strong> Read its job in the whole sentence.</li><li><strong>Identify both complete ideas.</strong> Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.</li><li><strong>Place the roles.</strong> Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.</li><li><strong>Write the contrapositive.</strong> Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.</li><li><strong>Read both in English.</strong> Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.</li></ol></div>\\r\\n      <p>The original and its contrapositive describe the same relationship. If writing both helps you see that relationship, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn\'t copy it merely to have a second line.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition\'s absence and work back to the left condition\'s absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={10} label=\\"Practical diagramming questions\\" title=\\"Use writing to make your reasoning easier to check\\">\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-faq\\">\\r\\n        <details><summary>When should I diagram?</summary><p>While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the relationship on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How do I check whether my diagram is right?</summary><p>Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>What if writing takes time?</summary><p>Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the sentences you are actually working through. First make the relationship accurate. Work on speed once you can explain why the arrow follows. </p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>Should I always write the contrapositive?</summary><p>You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.</p></details>\\r\\n        <aside className=\\"cr-training-note\\"><h3>Why this practice asks for both</h3><p>In the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We\'re checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.</p></aside>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How should I abbreviate the conditions?</summary><p>Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. W might mean win this raffle. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you\'re tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.</p></details>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?\\" options={[\'The labels need to be longer.\', \'The arrow makes the requirement enough.\', \'Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Longer labels would not repair the direction. The tray requires a paid stamp, but a stamped envelope can remain outside it.\', \'Only restricts tray envelopes to ones with paid stamps. In tray → Paid stamp preserves that requirement; the proposed reverse promises tray placement to every stamped envelope.\', \'Sentence order does not determine diagram order. Only identifies the paid stamp as required for tray placement.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>That error came from the language. Let\'s inspect precisely which idea an indicator refers to.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={11} label=\\"Find the target\\" title=\\"Read the whole idea the indicator refers to\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>“Editor approval is required for publication.”</p><p>“Publication requires editor approval.”</p><span>Same requirement, different placement</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Read which complete idea the sentence calls required. Then give that idea its role in the diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <p>When the wording still feels uncertain, we can test a proposed translation against an imagined case.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={12} label=\\"Check an interpretation\\" title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong> lets you test what a proposed sufficient condition would establish. First read the ordinary meaning. Propose the sufficient idea. Imagine a case meeting it. Ask what the sentence now requires.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Only people wearing helmets ride the studio bikes.</blockquote><p>The sentence restricts riders to people wearing helmets. Keep that meaning while trying each proposed starting point.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-personalized\\"><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">First proposed starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m riding a studio bike, what does this sentence tell me?\\" options={[\'I am wearing a helmet.\', \'The helmet question is open.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence puts every rider in the helmet-wearing group. Riding → Wearing helmet preserves that meaning.\', \'Only restricts who rides. If you meet the riding condition, the sentence does settle helmet wearing.\']}/></div><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Try the other starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m wearing a helmet, must I be riding a studio bike?\\" options={[\'Yes, the helmet proves riding.\', \'No, I could wear one without riding.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You gave every helmet wearer a ride. The sentence restricts riders; it does not make all helmet wearers riders.\', \'A helmet wearer waiting beside a bike fits the sentence. Wearing helmet → Riding would add a promise the original did not make.\']}/></div></div>\\r\\n      <p>The first reading matches the sentence. The second proposed arrow fails because it rules out a case the original permits. Use the imagined situation to inspect meaning, not to supply extra facts from what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Use the What Test beside it</h3><p>Ask a sensible question about the expression: only what? People wearing helmets. What is required? Wearing a helmet. What is enough? Riding a studio bike, under this rule, establishes that requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The What Test finds the complete target. The Personalized If/Then checks what your proposed direction says about a case. Use them together when one part of the translation is unclear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'ve now read the relationship, understood its limits, written both directions and checked the language. Put those pieces together with fresh sentences.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={13} label=\\"Use the whole process\\" title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n      <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You can look back. The job here is to produce and inspect the reasoning, not to prove you memorized every expression on the first reading. The labels below keep the complete ideas visible while you choose the directions.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-integrated-grid\\">{referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} key={item.sentence}/>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n    <section id=\\"reference-boundary\\" className=\\"cr-boundary co-section\\" data-reference-boundary data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Permission and instructions\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Before the attached practice</p><h2>Keep what the sentence actually gives you</h2>\\r\\n      <p>The core process is now in place. One more distinction matters for the practice: a conditional can give permission or an instruction. Preserve that strength instead of turning it into a report of what someone did.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote><p>May use gives permission here. A ticketed guest is allowed to use the cloakroom. The guest could keep their coat on and never go inside.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Lena is a guest with a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\" options={[\'Yes, the ticket establishes that she used it.\', \'No, we know she is allowed to use it.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned permission into a completed action. Lena meets the condition for permission, but we have no fact reporting her use of the cloakroom.\', \'The rule gives Lena permission because she is a guest with a ticket. Whether she used the cloakroom remains open.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Now compare an instruction. “If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack” tells you what to do with a wet coat. It does not report that someone followed the instruction.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice has not reported that anyone returned this tray.\', \'The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone returned it is a separate question.\', \'The instruction asks for a return. It does not say a return is impossible.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Allowed to use isn\'t used. Told to return isn\'t returned. Your conclusion should keep the meaning the sentence actually gave you.</p>\\r\\n    </section>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function ConditionalReferenceSequence() { return conditionalReferenceContent(); }\\r\\n\\r\\n// A long stage can contain several reading pages without changing the source order.\\r\\nexport function conditionalReferencePages(): LessonPage[] {\\r\\n  return Children.toArray(conditionalReferenceContent().props.children).flatMap(node => {\\r\\n    if (!isValidElement<{number?: number; title?: string; children?: ReactNode; \'data-toc-title\'?: string}>(node)) return [];\\r\\n    if (node.props.number === 8) {\\r\\n      const parts = Children.toArray(node.props.children);\\r\\n      const sentenceMove = parts.findIndex(child => isValidElement<{children?: ReactNode}>(child) && child.type === \'h3\' && child.props.children === \'The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.\');\\r\\n      const targetMove = parts.findIndex(child => isValidElement<{children?: ReactNode}>(child) && child.type === \'h3\' && child.props.children === \'The indicator can also follow its target\');\\r\\n      return [\\r\\n        { title: \'Build the first diagram: the two sides\', content: <ReferenceStep number={8} label=\\"Build the first diagram · 1 of 3\\" title=\\"Give the relationship a shorter form\\">{parts.slice(0, sentenceMove)}</ReferenceStep> },\\r\\n        { title: \'Build the first diagram: sentence order\', content: <ReferenceStep number={8} part={2} label=\\"Build the first diagram · 2 of 3\\" title=\\"The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.\\">{parts.slice(sentenceMove + 1, targetMove)}</ReferenceStep> },\\r\\n        { title: \'Build the first diagram: target and readback\', content: <ReferenceStep number={8} part={3} label=\\"Build the first diagram · 3 of 3\\" title=\\"Find the target wherever it appears\\">{parts.slice(targetMove + 1)}</ReferenceStep> },\\r\\n        { title: \'Recall enough and required\', content: <section className=\\"co-section\\"><h2>Recall enough and required</h2><p>Put the worked examples aside. Read each new rule, say your answer, and then turn the card to compare your reasoning.</p><StudyDeck group=\\"foundation\\"/></section> },\\r\\n      ];\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    if (node.props.number === 9) {\\r\\n      return [\\r\\n        { title: node.props.title!, content: node },\\r\\n        { title: \'Two possible rooms, one limit\', content: <section className=\\"co-section\\"><h2>Two possible rooms, one limit</h2><NegativeCaseLab/></section> },\\r\\n      ];\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    if (node.props.number === 13) {\\r\\n      return referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => ({\\r\\n        title: `Use the whole process: sentence ${index + 1}`,\\r\\n        content: <ReferenceStep number={13} part={index > 0 ? index + 1 : undefined} label={`Use the whole process · ${index + 1} of ${referencePracticeItems.length}`} title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n          {index === 0 && <p>For each new sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. You can return to any earlier page.</p>}\\r\\n          <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1}/>\\r\\n          <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n        </ReferenceStep>,\\r\\n      }));\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    return [{ title: node.props.title || node.props[\'data-toc-title\'] || \'Keep the meaning\', content: node }];\\r\\nimport { useId, useLayoutEffect, useRef, useState, type ReactNode } from \'react\';\\r\\nimport { Link } from \'react-router-dom\';\\r\\nimport { LessonPages, flattenLessonPages } from \'./LessonPages\';\\r\\nimport { StudyDeck } from \'./StudyDeck\';\\r\\nimport { CounterexampleLab, RuleComparisonLab } from \'./CounterexampleLab\';\\r\\nimport { ConditionalOpeningMedia } from \'./ConditionalOpeningMedia\';\\r\\nimport { GROUPS } from \'@/features/conditional-refresher/content\';\\r\\nimport type { ConditionalLessonNoteProps } from \'./ConditionalLessonNote\';\\r\\nimport \'./opening.css\';\\r\\nimport { conditionalReferencePages } from \'./ConditionalReferenceSequence\';\\r\\nimport \'./opening-polish.css\';\\r\\n\\r\\nexport const hasOpeningLesson = (id: string) => [\'orientation\', \'sufficient-necessary\', \'group-1\', \'group-2\'].includes(id);\\r\\nfunction Section({ title, children, dark = false, expressionNumber, id }: { title: string; children: ReactNode; dark?: boolean; expressionNumber?: number; id?: string }) {\\r\\n  const tocTitle = title.includes(\':\') ? title.split(\':\')[0] : ({\\r\\n    \'Find the target, then place it\': \'Find the target\',\\r\\n    \'Find the requirement, then place it\': \'Find the requirement\',\\r\\n    \'Try the sentence from the inside\': \'Personalized If/Then\',\\r\\n    \'A guarantee is stronger than a tendency\': \'Guarantees and wording\',\\r\\n    \'Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\': \'Requirement or guarantee?\',\\r\\n    \'Try the same reasoning with a new rule\': \'Try a new rule\',\\r\\n    \'Say the rule without the clues\': \'Recall the rule\',\\r\\n  }[title] || title);\\r\\n  return <section id={id || `co-${title.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]+/g, \'-\')}`} className={`co-section ${dark ? \'co-dark\' : \'\'} ${expressionNumber ? \'co-expression-section\' : \'\'}`} data-toc data-toc-title={tocTitle}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-section-heading\\">{expressionNumber && <span className=\\"co-expression-number\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\">{String(expressionNumber).padStart(2, \'0\')}</span>}<h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction Arrow({ left, right }: { left: string; right: string }) {\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-arrow\\"><span>{left}</span><b aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</b><span className=\\"co-sr-only\\">implies</span><span>{right}</span></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction ThinkPause({ prompt, children }: { prompt: string; children: ReactNode }) {\\r\\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const answerId = useId();\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-think-pause\\"><h3 id={`${answerId}-prompt`}>{prompt}</h3><button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-primary\\" aria-describedby={`${answerId}-prompt`} aria-expanded={revealed} aria-controls={answerId} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? \'Hide the reasoning\' : \'Show the reasoning\'}</button><div id={answerId} aria-live=\\"polite\\">{revealed && <div className=\\"co-pause-answer\\">{children}</div>}</div></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction Check({ prompt, options, correct, feedback }: { prompt: string; options: string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[] }) {\\r\\n  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<number | null>(null);\\r\\n  if (!Array.isArray(feedback) || feedback.length !== options.length || feedback.some(message => typeof message !== \'string\' || !message.trim())) {\\r\\n    throw new Error(`Every answer choice needs an explanation: ${prompt}`);\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-check\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Try it</p><h3>{prompt}</h3><div className=\\"co-choices\\">{options.map((option, i) => <button key={option} type=\\"button\\" aria-pressed={choice === i} onClick={() => setChoice(i)}>{option}</button>)}</div><div role=\\"status\\">{choice !== null && <div className={`co-feedback ${choice === correct ? \'co-correct\' : \'\'}`} ><span className=\\"co-answer-state\\">{choice === correct ? \'Correct.\' : \'Not quite.\'}</span><strong>Your choice: {options[choice]}</strong><p>{feedback[choice]}</p></div>}</div></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction GateExplorer() {\\r\\n  const [fact, setFact] = useState<\'win\' | \'bicycle\'>(\'win\');\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-gate\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Enough and required</p><Arrow left=\\"Win the raffle\\" right=\\"Get a bicycle\\"/><div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button aria-pressed={fact === \'win\'} onClick={() => setFact(\'win\')}>You win the raffle</button><button aria-pressed={fact === \'bicycle\'} onClick={() => setFact(\'bicycle\')}>You get a bicycle</button></div><div className=\\"co-gate-track\\"><div className={fact === \'win\' ? \'co-lit\' : \'\'}>Win the raffle<span>Sufficient: enough</span></div><b aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</b><span className=\\"co-sr-only\\">implies</span><div className=\\"co-lit\\">Get a bicycle<span>Necessary: required</span></div></div><p role=\\"status\\">{fact === \'win\' ? \'You get a bicycle because the rule gives you that result if you win.\' : \'You get a bicycle. Winning remains unknown: you could have bought it or received it as a gift instead.\'}</p></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\ntype Translation = { word: string; sentence: string; target: string; other: string; left: string; right: string; why: string; readback: string };\\r\\ntype MappedTranslation = Translation & { sentenceTarget: string };\\r\\ntype DiagramTranslation = MappedTranslation & { reversedPromise: string };\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function orderedConditionalIdeas(item: Pick<Translation, \'sentence\' | \'target\' | \'other\'>): [string, string] {\\r\\n  let hash = 2166136261;\\r\\nimport { useId, useState } from \'react\';\\r\\n\\r\\nconst rooms = [\\r\\n  { film: true, workshop: false, label: \'Film showing. No workshop.\' },\\r\\n  { film: false, workshop: false, label: \'No film. No workshop.\' },\\r\\n  { film: true, workshop: true, label: \'Film showing. Workshop underway.\' },\\r\\n];\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function NegativeCaseLab() {\\r\\n  const [selected, setSelected] = useState<number[]>([]);\\r\\n  const [checked, setChecked] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const [workshopAnswer, setWorkshopAnswer] = useState<boolean | null>(null);\\r\\n  const id = useId();\\r\\n  const correct = selected.length === 2 && selected.includes(0) && selected.includes(1);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-negative-lab\\">\\r\\n    <p>Let\'s take the same reasoning into a sentence whose required condition contains a negative. Accept this rule for the exercise:</p>\\r\\n    <blockquote>If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop.</blockquote>\\r\\n    <p>Use <strong>F</strong> for showing a film and <strong>W</strong> for hosting a workshop. Our rule is <strong>F → ~W</strong>. A film requires the absence of a workshop. We haven\'t said a film is the only reason a room might have no workshop.</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-given-fact\\"><span className=\\"co-kicker\\">Given fact</span><strong>This room is not hosting a workshop: ~W.</strong></div>\\r\\n    <h3 id={id}>Which possible rooms fit both the rule and that fact? Select every one that fits.</h3>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-room-options\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-labelledby={id}>{rooms.map((room, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={room.label} aria-pressed={selected.includes(index)} onClick={() => { setSelected(items => items.includes(index) ? items.filter(i => i !== index) : [...items, index]); setChecked(false); }}><span className=\\"co-room-drawing\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><i data-lit={room.film}/><b data-active={room.workshop}>W</b></span><span>{room.label}</span></button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-primary\\" disabled={selected.length === 0} onClick={() => setChecked(true)}>Check these possibilities</button>\\r\\n    <div role=\\"status\\">{checked && <div className=\\"co-feedback\\"><strong>{correct ? \'Both film possibilities remain open.\' : selected.includes(2) ? \'A workshop conflicts with the given fact.\' : \'There is another case that fits.\'}</strong><p>{selected.includes(2) ? \\"The fact says no workshop. A film and a workshop would also break the original rule. Remove that room, then check the others.\\" : \\"A film with no workshop fits. No film with no workshop fits too. Both keep the rule true and satisfy the given fact, so ~W establishes neither F nor ~F. We know there is no workshop; we don\'t know whether there is a film.\\"}</p></div>}</div>\\r\\n    {checked && correct && <div className=\\"co-case-takeaway\\"><h3>Change the fact: now a workshop is underway.</h3><p>The rule stays F → ~W. Could this room also be showing a film?</p><div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button type=\\"button\\" aria-pressed={workshopAnswer === true} onClick={() => setWorkshopAnswer(true)}>Yes, it could.</button><button type=\\"button\\" aria-pressed={workshopAnswer === false} onClick={() => setWorkshopAnswer(false)}>No, it could not.</button></div><div role=\\"status\\">{workshopAnswer !== null && <p>{workshopAnswer ? \\"A film would require no workshop. That conflicts with our new fact that a workshop is underway, so a film is ruled out.\\" : \\"A film would require no workshop, but a workshop is underway. That rules out a film. In symbols, W → ~F. Negating ~W gives W, which is why this is the contrapositive.\\"}</p>}</div></div>}\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst comparisons = [\\r\\n  { sentence: \'Only stamped envelopes go into the dispatch tray.\', same: true, reason: \'Only makes a stamp necessary for an envelope in the tray. An envelope in the tray must be stamped. A stamped envelope can still be elsewhere.\' },\\r\\n  { sentence: \'An envelope goes into the dispatch tray if it is stamped.\', same: false, reason: \'If now makes the stamp sufficient for going into the tray. This sends every stamped envelope there. What is required?\';\\r\\n  const repairTitle = orientation ? \\"Don\'t turn a rule into a case fact\\" : unit.id === \'sufficient-necessary\' ? \\"Don\'t turn a requirement into a guarantee\\" : unit.id === \'group-1\' ? \'Keep the direction and the whole condition\' : \'Keep requirements and completed actions distinct\';\\r\\n  const subtitle = orientation\\r\\n    ? \\"A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too. [{ title: \'Return to the relationship\', content: <ConditionalOpeningMedia unitId={unit.id}/> }] : []),\\r\\n        ...(orientation ? It has not said winning is the only route. When that sufficient condition is absent, the other condition may still be true.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. If you win, you have to get a bicycle. Getting one is the required result. Here it is a required result of winning, not a task you must complete before entering the raffle.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required whenever you win, under this rule. The relationship gives the word its meaning. The word necessary does not tell you which event happens first.</p>\\r\\n"}'}]

Their stateful exercises are\n+// mounted by React; the former reader-page renderer is never invoked here.\n+function sections(children: ReactNode): ContentSection[] {\n+  return Children.toArray(children).flatMap(child => {\n+    if (!isValidElement<ContentSection['props']>(child)) return [];\n+    return child.type === Fragment ? sections(child.props.children) : [child];\n+  });\n+}\n+\n+function reference(number: number): ContentSection {\n+  const section = sections(conditionalReferenceContent().props.children).find(item => item.props.number === number);\n+  if (!section) throw new Error(`Missing approved conditional reference section ${number}`);\n+  return section;\n+}\n+\n+function groupSections(group: 'group-1' | 'group-2') {\n+  return sections(GroupLessons({ id: group }).props.children);\n+}\n+\n+function groupSection(group: 'group-1' | 'group-2', title: string) {\n+  const section = groupSections(group).find(item => item.props.title === title);\n+  if (!section) throw new Error(`Missing approved conditional section: ${title}`);\n+  return section;\n+}\n+\n+function NoteSection({ title, children }: { title: string; children: ReactNode }) {\n+  return <section className=\"co-section\"><div className=\"co-section-heading\"><h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}</section>;\n+}\n+\n+function ReferenceBody({ number, omitTransition = false }: { number: number; omitTransition?: boolean }) {\n+  const source = reference(number);\n+  const children = Children.toArray(source.props.children);\n+  // These source sections end in navigation prose for the former ordering.\n+  // The course shell now owns transitions; the teaching stays in its source.\n+  return <section className={`co-section cr-course-section ${source.props.dark ? 'cr-dark' : ''}`} data-reference-source={number}>\n+    <div className=\"co-section-heading\"><h2>{source.props.title}</h2></div>\n+    <div className=\"cr-step-body\">{omitTransition ? children.slice(0, -1) : children}</div>\n+  </section>;\n+}\n+\n+function FamilySupplement({ group }: { group: 'group-1' | 'group-2' }) {\n+  const source = reference(group === 'group-1' ? 3 : 5);\n+  const family = sections(source.props.children).find(child => child.type === WordFamily);\n+  if (!family) throw new Error(`Missing approved ${group} supplementary family`);\n+  return <details className=\"co-practical-note\"><summary>More expressions for the same relationship</summary>\n+    <p>Use the stated rule: anyone who borrows a locker has a key. These sentences preserve that connection. They do not say every person with a key borrows a locker.</p>\n+    {family}\n+  </details>;\n+}\n+\n+function IndicatorNote({ group }: { group: 'group-1' | 'group-2' }) {\n+  // Two introductions and the seven complete expression explanations belong\n+  // together in one concept note, not nine separate navigation destinations.\n+  return <>{groupSections(group).slice(0, 9)}<FamilySupplement group={group}/>\n+    <NoteSection title=\"Recall the expressions\"><p>Explain the relationship before turning each card. Compare the complete ideas and the direction with your answer.</p><StudyDeck group={group}/></NoteSection>\n+  </>;\n+}\n+\n+function FoundationPractice() {\n+  const definitionQuestions = [1, 2, 4].flatMap(number => sections(reference(number).props.children).filter(child => child.type === ReferenceQuestion));\n+  const fresh = sections(Fundamentals().props.children).find(section => section.props.title === 'Enough does not mean the only way');\n+  if (!fresh) throw new Error('Missing approved fresh sufficient-condition check');\n+  const freshCheck = Children.toArray(fresh.props.children).at(-1);\n+  return <>\n+    <NoteSection title=\"Separate the rule from the fact\"><blockquote>If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</blockquote><p>Use the rule to decide what each fact establishes. A wrong answer is a reason to inspect the connection and try again.</p>{definitionQuestions}</NoteSection>\n+    <NoteSection title=\"Try a fresh case\">{freshCheck}</NoteSection>\n+    <NoteSection title=\"Build a case that proves the limit\"><CounterexampleLab/></NoteSection>\n+  </>;\n+}\n+\n+function GroupPractice({ group }: { group: 'group-1' | 'group-2' }) {\n+  return <>\n+    {groupSection(group, group === 'group-1' ? 'Find the target, then place it' : 'Find the requirement, then place it')}\n+    {groupSection(group, 'From sentence to diagram')}\n+    {groupSection(group, 'Check the meaning, not just the word')}\n+    <details className=\"co-practical-note\"><summary>More sentences to recall and explain</summary>{groupSection(group, 'Say the rule without the clues')}</details>\n+  </>;\n+}\n+\n+function ContrapositivePractice() {\n+  const [current, setCurrent] = useState(0);\n+  return <NoteSection title=\"Build an original and its contrapositive\">\n+    <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. If an answer needs revision, inspect the direction, qualifications and negation, then retry.</p>\n+    <p className=\"co-kicker\" role=\"status\">Sentence {current + 1} of {referencePracticeItems.length}</p>\n+    {referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <div key={item.sentence} hidden={current !== index} data-course-practice-item={index + 1}><IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1}/></div>)}\n+    <div className=\"co-choices\"><button type=\"button\" disabled={current === 0} onClick={() => setCurrent(current - 1)}>Previous sentence</button><button type=\"button\" disabled={current === referencePracticeItems.length - 1} onClick={() => setCurrent(current + 1)}>Next sentence</button></div>\n+    <p>Moving between sentences keeps your choices and feedback. Now make that limit visible.</p>\\r\\n    <blockquote>Every ticket in Drawer R has a blue border.</blockquote>\\r\\n    <p>Accept that rule for this exercise. We don\'t need to know why the tickets were arranged that way. A ticket in Drawer R must have a blue border.</p>\\r\\n    <h3 id={id}>Someone claims: every blue-bordered ticket is in Drawer R.
